<a id='question-110'></a>

### Question 110: Hematopoietic Pathology: Leukemias and Lymphomas: Focus 03-10 (Microbiology)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the understanding of the pathogenesis, clinical presentation, and diagnostic features of Epstein-Barr Virus (EBV)-associated lymphoproliferative disorders, specifically Burkitt lymphoma, and its association with endemic malaria. (考察EBV相关淋巴增殖性疾病，特别是伯基特淋巴瘤的发病机制、临床表现和诊断特征，以及其与地方性疟疾的关联。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 7-year-old boy living in a rural area of equatorial Africa presents with a rapidly enlarging mass in his jaw, causing facial swelling and difficulty eating. He has experienced intermittent fevers and night sweats for the past month. Physical examination reveals a firm, non-tender mass involving the maxilla and mandible, along with generalized lymphadenopathy. His spleen is palpable 5 cm below the costal margin. Laboratory tests show anemia (Hb 8 g/dL), thrombocytopenia (platelets 50,000/µL), and elevated lactate dehydrogenase (LDH 800 U/L). A biopsy of the jaw mass shows a diffuse proliferation of medium-sized lymphoid cells with round nuclei, finely clumped chromatin, multiple small nucleoli, and scant cytoplasm. The cells are negative for CD20 and CD10, but strongly positive for CD30 and CD45. Cytogenetic analysis reveals a translocation t(8;14)(q24;q32). The patient's village reports a recent increase in malaria cases.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying factor contributing to the development of this patient's condition?

(A) Chronic infection with Helicobacter pylori leading to gastric MALT lymphoma.
(B) Immunosuppression secondary to HIV infection facilitating the development of diffuse large B-cell lymphoma (DLBCL).
(C) Impaired humoral immunity due to chronic malaria infection predisposing to EBV-driven Burkitt lymphoma.
(D) Genetic predisposition involving mutations in the TP53 gene leading to Li-Fraumeni syndrome and subsequent development of osteosarcoma.
(E) Autoimmune destruction of B lymphocytes secondary to systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) leading to immunodeficiency and opportunistic infections.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 7-year-old boy from equatorial Africa. This geographic location is crucial, suggesting endemic diseases like malaria and EBV.
*   **Presenting Symptoms:** Rapidly enlarging jaw mass, fever, night sweats, difficulty eating. These point towards a rapidly proliferating malignancy, likely lymphoma, affecting the jaw (a common site for Burkitt lymphoma).
*   **Physical Exam:** Jaw mass, generalized lymphadenopathy, splenomegaly. Consistent with widespread lymphoid malignancy.
*   **Laboratory Findings:** Anemia, thrombocytopenia, elevated LDH. Suggestive of bone marrow involvement or high tumor burden, common in aggressive lymphomas like Burkitt lymphoma. LDH is a marker of cell turnover.
*   **Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse proliferation of medium-sized lymphoid cells with characteristic "starry sky" appearance (implied by the description: round nuclei, clumped chromatin, small nucleoli, scant cytoplasm, often seen with macrophages engulfing apoptotic cells). Immunophenotype: CD20 negative, CD10 negative, CD30 positive, CD45 positive. This immunophenotype is atypical for classic Burkitt lymphoma (which is usually CD20+, CD10+, CD30-) but can be seen in variants or other aggressive lymphomas. However, the cytogenetics are key.
*   **Cytogenetics:** t(8;14)(q24;q32). This is the classic translocation associated with Burkitt lymphoma, involving the MYC oncogene on chromosome 8 and the immunoglobulin heavy chain locus on chromosome 14, leading to MYC overexpression.
*   **Epidemiology:** Village reports increased malaria cases. This links the patient's condition to the endemic environment.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying factor contributing to the condition.

The constellation of findings (age, location, jaw mass, rapid growth, cytogenetics t(8;14)) strongly points towards Burkitt lymphoma. The association with increased malaria cases in the region suggests a link between chronic malaria infection and the development of EBV-associated Burkitt lymphoma. Chronic malaria impairs humoral immunity, specifically B-cell responses, making individuals more susceptible to EBV infection and subsequent malignant transformation.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Burkitt Lymphoma Pathogenesis:** Burkitt lymphoma is a highly aggressive B-cell non-Hodgkin lymphoma. The hallmark genetic abnormality is the t(8;14) translocation, which juxtaposes the MYC proto-oncogene (chromosome 8) with immunoglobulin heavy chain gene enhancers (chromosome 14). This leads to constitutive overexpression of MYC, a transcription factor that drives cell proliferation and apoptosis inhibition.
*   **EBV Association:** EBV is strongly associated with endemic Burkitt lymphoma, particularly in equatorial Africa. EBV infects B lymphocytes and contributes to their immortalization and proliferation.
*   **Malaria and Burkitt Lymphoma Link:** Chronic malaria infection, particularly Plasmodium falciparum, is endemic in the regions where Burkitt lymphoma is most common. Malaria infection impairs the development of effective anti-EBV antibody responses (humoral immunity). This impaired immunity allows for persistent EBV infection and increases the risk of EBV-driven B-cell proliferation and malignant transformation into Burkitt lymphoma. The mechanism involves chronic antigenic stimulation by malaria parasites, leading to B-cell activation and exhaustion, and potentially altered T-cell regulation of EBV-infected B cells.
*   **Immunophenotype Note:** While classic Burkitt lymphoma is CD20+, CD10+, CD30-, the description provided (CD20-, CD10-, CD30+) might represent a variant or a misinterpretation/oversimplification for the question. However, the t(8;14) translocation is definitive for Burkitt lymphoma. The question focuses on the *underlying factor* contributing to its development in this specific context.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Chronic infection with Helicobacter pylori leading to gastric MALT lymphoma:** H. pylori infection is associated with MALT (Mucosa-Associated Lymphoid Tissue) lymphoma, typically in the stomach. This lymphoma is usually low-grade and indolent, unlike the rapidly progressive jaw mass described. The location (jaw vs. stomach) and clinical course are inconsistent. MALT lymphoma is also typically associated with t(11;18) translocation involving BCL10 and API2 genes, not t(8;14).
- (B) **Immunosuppression secondary to HIV infection facilitating the development of diffuse large B-cell lymphoma (DLBCL):** HIV infection causes immunosuppression, increasing the risk of various lymphomas, including DLBCL and Burkitt lymphoma. However, the vignette describes a child in a malaria-endemic region, not explicitly mentioning HIV risk factors. While HIV can cause Burkitt lymphoma, the strong epidemiological link to malaria in this specific context makes option (C) more likely. DLBCL typically has a different morphology and immunophenotype (usually CD20+, CD10 variable, CD30 often negative) and genetic profile (often involving BCL2, BCL6, or MYC rearrangements, but not typically the classic t(8;14) as the defining feature).
- (D) **Genetic predisposition involving mutations in the TP53 gene leading to Li-Fraumeni syndrome and subsequent development of osteosarcoma:** Li-Fraumeni syndrome is an inherited cancer predisposition syndrome caused by germline TP53 mutations. It increases the risk of various cancers, including sarcomas (like osteosarcoma), breast cancer, brain tumors, and adrenocortical carcinoma. While lymphomas can occur, it's not the primary association, and the specific genetic translocation t(8;14) is not characteristic of Li-Fraumeni syndrome. The clinical presentation (jaw mass, cytogenetics) is inconsistent with osteosarcoma.
- (E) **Autoimmune destruction of B lymphocytes secondary to systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) leading to immunodeficiency and opportunistic infections:** SLE is an autoimmune disease that can cause lymphopenia due to B-cell destruction or dysfunction. This immunodeficiency can increase susceptibility to infections, including EBV. However, SLE itself does not directly cause Burkitt lymphoma. While EBV is associated with SLE, the primary link between malaria, impaired B-cell immunity, and EBV-driven Burkitt lymphoma in this endemic setting is a well-established phenomenon, making (C) the most direct and likely underlying factor described in the vignette.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | Burkitt Lymphoma (Endemic) | Gastric MALT Lymphoma | HIV-associated DLBCL | Li-Fraumeni Syndrome | SLE-related Immunodeficiency |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------ | :-------------------- | :------------------- | :------------------- | :--------------------------- |
| **Primary Cause/Risk**  | EBV + Malaria (impaired humoral immunity) | H. pylori infection | HIV immunosuppression | TP53 germline mutation | Autoimmune B-cell destruction |
| **Typical Location**     | Jaw, abdomen              | Stomach              | Various (nodes, extranodal) | Various (bone, soft tissue) | N/A                          |
| **Clinical Course**     | Rapidly progressive       | Indolent             | Variable (often aggressive) | Variable             | N/A                          |
| **Hallmark Genetics**   | t(8;14)                   | t(11;18)              | Variable (MYC, BCL2, etc.) | TP53 mutation       | N/A                          |
| **EBV Association**     | Strong (endemic)          | Rare                 | Increased risk       | Not primary         | Increased risk of EBV infection |
| **Immunophenotype (Classic)** | CD20+, CD10+, CD30- | CD19+, CD22+, CD79a+ | CD20+, CD10 variable | N/A                 | N/A                          |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The endemic form of Burkitt lymphoma in equatorial Africa is strongly associated with EBV infection and chronic malaria. Malaria impairs the development of effective anti-EBV humoral immunity, increasing the risk of EBV-driven B-cell lymphomagenesis. Key differentials include other aggressive lymphomas (DLBCL), MALT lymphoma, and cancers associated with genetic syndromes like Li-Fraumeni. Always consider the geographic location and endemic diseases when evaluating lymphoma cases.

<a id='question-111'></a>

### Question 111: Hematopoietic Pathology: Leukemias and Lymphomas: Focus 03-11 (Behavioral Science/Social Sciences)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the clinical presentation, diagnostic criteria, and specific cytogenetic abnormalities associated with Acute Promyelocytic Leukemia (APL), particularly the role of t(15;17) translocation and its impact on treatment response to all-trans retinoic acid (ATRA). It also assesses the recognition of differentiation syndrome, a common complication of APL treatment. (考察急性早幼粒细胞白血病 (APL) 的临床表现、诊断标准、特异性细胞遗传学异常 t(15;17) 及其对全反式维甲酸 (ATRA) 治疗反应的影响，以及鉴别 APL 治疗中的一种常见并发症——分化综合征。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the emergency department with a 2-week history of fatigue, fever, and easy bruising. He denies any significant past medical history or family history of hematologic malignancies. Physical examination reveals pallor, multiple petechiae on the upper extremities, and mild splenomegaly. Initial laboratory results show pancytopenia (WBC 1.2 x 10^9/L, Hb 8.5 g/dL, Platelets 25 x 10^9/L). A peripheral blood smear reveals a high percentage of abnormal promyelocytes with Auer rods. Bone marrow biopsy confirms the diagnosis of acute myeloid leukemia (AML). Cytogenetic analysis of the bone marrow cells reveals the t(15;17) translocation. The patient is immediately started on all-trans retinoic acid (ATRA) and arsenic trioxide (ATO). Three days into treatment, the patient develops fever, dyspnea, hypoxia, and bilateral pulmonary infiltrates on chest X-ray. Laboratory tests show leukocytosis, elevated C-reactive protein (CRP), and mild renal impairment.

Which of the following is the most likely explanation for the patient's current clinical presentation?

(A) Tumor lysis syndrome secondary to rapid cytoreduction from chemotherapy.
(B) Differentiation syndrome resulting from the cytotoxic effects of ATO.
(C) Infection secondary to profound neutropenia caused by the underlying AML.
(D) Acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS) due to pulmonary hemorrhage from thrombocytopenia.
(E) Allergic reaction to ATRA manifesting as pulmonary edema.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old male with fatigue, fever, bruising (suggesting cytopenias).
- **Physical Exam:** Pallor (anemia), petechiae (thrombocytopenia), mild splenomegaly (common in leukemia).
- **Labs:** Pancytopenia (WBC, Hb, Platelets low), confirming bone marrow failure.
- **Peripheral Smear:** Abnormal promyelocytes with Auer rods (hallmark of APL).
- **Bone Marrow Biopsy:** Confirms AML.
- **Cytogenetics:** t(15;17) translocation (diagnostic for APL).
- **Treatment:** ATRA and ATO (standard therapy for APL).
- **Complication (3 days post-treatment):** Fever, dyspnea, hypoxia, pulmonary infiltrates, leukocytosis, elevated CRP, mild renal impairment. This constellation of symptoms occurring shortly after initiating ATRA/ATO therapy in a patient with APL is highly suggestive of differentiation syndrome.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **APL Pathogenesis:** The t(15;17) translocation fuses the PML gene on chromosome 15 with the RARA (retinoic acid receptor alpha) gene on chromosome 17, creating the PML-RARA fusion protein. This fusion protein blocks myeloid differentiation at the promyelocyte stage, leading to an accumulation of these immature cells in the bone marrow and periphery.
- **ATRA Mechanism:** ATRA binds to the PML-RARA fusion protein, inducing a conformational change that releases the block on differentiation. This leads to the rapid differentiation of malignant promyelocytes into mature granulocytes.
- **Differentiation Syndrome:** This is a potentially life-threatening complication of APL treatment, particularly with ATRA. The rapid differentiation and release of cellular contents (cytokines, enzymes, granules) from the leukemic cells cause a systemic inflammatory response. Clinical features include fever, dyspnea, hypoxia, pulmonary infiltrates, pleural effusions, pericardial effusions, weight gain, hypotension, renal failure, and sometimes disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC). The leukocytosis and elevated CRP seen in the patient are characteristic inflammatory markers. The pulmonary manifestations are due to capillary leak and inflammatory infiltrates.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Tumor lysis syndrome (TLS) typically occurs after initiation of cytotoxic chemotherapy, especially in high-burden malignancies like ALL or Burkitt lymphoma. It is characterized by hyperkalemia, hyperphosphatemia, hypocalcemia, hyperuricemia, and acute kidney injury due to the rapid breakdown of tumor cells. While ATRA/ATO can cause cytoreduction, TLS is less common in APL compared to other leukemias, and the specific constellation of symptoms (fever, dyspnea, pulmonary infiltrates, leukocytosis) is more typical of differentiation syndrome. The timing (3 days) is also more consistent with differentiation syndrome than typical TLS, which often peaks earlier.
- (C): Infection is a major concern in AML patients due to neutropenia. However, the patient's presentation with fever, dyspnea, hypoxia, pulmonary infiltrates, leukocytosis (not neutropenia), and elevated CRP points towards a systemic inflammatory process rather than a typical bacterial infection, although infection can sometimes trigger or coexist with differentiation syndrome. The leukocytosis is particularly atypical for a simple infection in a neutropenic patient.
- (D): Pulmonary hemorrhage can occur in AML, especially with severe thrombocytopenia and DIC. However, the patient's presentation includes fever, leukocytosis, and elevated CRP, which are not typical features of isolated pulmonary hemorrhage. While thrombocytopenia is present, the overall clinical picture strongly favors differentiation syndrome.
- (E): Allergic reactions to ATRA can occur but typically manifest as rash, urticaria, or anaphylaxis. Pulmonary edema can occur in severe reactions, but fever, leukocytosis, and elevated CRP are not characteristic features of an allergic reaction. The timing and specific symptoms are much more consistent with differentiation syndrome.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | Differentiation Syndrome (B) | Tumor Lysis Syndrome (A) | Infection (C) | Pulmonary Hemorrhage (D) | Allergic Reaction (E) |
|--------------------------|-----------------------------|--------------------------|---------------|--------------------------|-----------------------|
| **Timing**               | Days after ATRA initiation | Early post-chemo        | Any time     | Any time                 | Early post-drug       |
| **Fever**                | Common                      | Uncommon                 | Common        | Uncommon                 | Uncommon              |
| **Dyspnea/Hypoxia**      | Common                      | Uncommon                 | Common        | Common                   | Common                |
| **Pulmonary Infiltrates**| Common                      | Uncommon                 | Common        | Uncommon                 | Uncommon              |
| **Leukocytosis**         | Common                      | Uncommon                 | Uncommon      | Uncommon                 | Uncommon              |
| **Elevated CRP**         | Common                      | Uncommon                 | Common        | Uncommon                 | Uncommon              |
| **Weight Gain**          | Common                      | Uncommon                 | Uncommon      | Uncommon                 | Uncommon              |
| **Renal Impairment**     | Common                      | Common                   | Uncommon      | Uncommon                 | Uncommon              |
| **Underlying Cause**     | Rapid differentiation       | Cell lysis               | Pathogen     | Thrombocytopenia/DIC     | IgE-mediated          |
| **Specific to APL?**     | Yes                         | No                       | No           | No                       | No                    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Differentiation syndrome is a common and potentially fatal complication of APL treatment with ATRA, characterized by fever, respiratory distress, pulmonary infiltrates, and signs of systemic inflammation due to rapid leukemic cell differentiation. It must be differentiated from infection, TLS, pulmonary hemorrhage, and allergic reactions. (APL治疗中，分化综合征是一种常见且潜在致命的并发症，其特征是发烧、呼吸窘、肺浸润和全身炎症迹象，这是由于恶性早幼粒细胞的快速分化所致。必须将其与感染、肿瘤溶解综合征、肺出血和过敏反应相鉴别。)

<a id='question-112'></a>

### Question 112: Hematopoietic Pathology: Leukemias and Lymphomas
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of Burkitt lymphoma, specifically its characteristic morphology, genetic translocation, clinical presentation (often rapid growth and specific locations), and association with Epstein-Barr virus (EBV). 伯基特淋巴瘤的形态学特征、基因易位、临床表现（快速生长和特定部位）以及与EB病毒（EBV）的关联。

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 25-year-old male from Equatorial Africa presents with a rapidly enlarging mass in his jaw, causing facial asymmetry and difficulty chewing. He also reports intermittent fevers and night sweats over the past month. Physical examination reveals a firm, non-tender mass involving the mandible, along with generalized lymphadenopathy, particularly in the cervical and axillary regions. A biopsy of the jaw mass shows a diffuse proliferation of medium-sized lymphoid cells with round nuclei, finely clumped chromatin, prominent nucleoli, and scant cytoplasm. The cells are interspersed with numerous tangible body macrophages (TBMs) and have a high mitotic rate. Immunohistochemistry is positive for CD20, CD10, BCL6, and MUM1, but negative for BCL2. Cytogenetic analysis reveals a translocation involving the MYC gene on chromosome 8 and the immunoglobulin heavy chain locus on chromosome 14, t(8;14)(q24;q32).

Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis?

(A) Diffuse large B-cell lymphoma (DLBCL)
(B) Follicular lymphoma
(C) Mantle cell lymphoma
(D) Burkitt lymphoma
(E) Hodgkin lymphoma

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Demographics & Location:** Young adult male from Equatorial Africa. This geographic location is endemic for Burkitt lymphoma, particularly the African (endemic) variant, often associated with EBV and presenting as jaw or facial bone involvement.
*   **Rapidly Enlarging Jaw Mass:** This is a classic presentation for the endemic variant of Burkitt lymphoma.
*   **Systemic Symptoms:** Intermittent fevers and night sweats (B symptoms) are common in aggressive lymphomas.
*   **Physical Exam:** Firm, non-tender jaw mass and generalized lymphadenopathy are consistent with lymphoma.
*   **Histopathology:** Diffuse proliferation of medium-sized lymphoid cells with round nuclei, finely clumped chromatin (often described as "starry sky" appearance due to TBMs), prominent nucleoli, scant cytoplasm, high mitotic rate, and numerous tangible body macrophages (TBMs). This morphology is highly characteristic of Burkitt lymphoma.
*   **Immunohistochemistry:** CD20+ (B-cell marker), CD10+ (germinal center marker), BCL6+ (germinal center marker), MUM1+ (plasma cell differentiation marker, often positive in Burkitt), BCL2- (important negative marker, distinguishes it from follicular lymphoma).
*   **Cytogenetics:** t(8;14)(q24;q32) involving the MYC gene and the immunoglobulin heavy chain locus. This translocation leads to MYC overexpression, a hallmark of Burkitt lymphoma.

All these features strongly point towards Burkitt lymphoma.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Burkitt Lymphoma Pathogenesis:** Burkitt lymphoma is a highly aggressive B-cell non-Hodgkin lymphoma. The defining genetic event is a translocation involving the MYC proto-oncogene (located at 8q24) and one of the immunoglobulin loci (heavy chain at 14q32, kappa light chain at 2p12, or lambda light chain at 22q11). The most common translocation is t(8;14)(q24;q32). This translocation places the MYC gene under the control of strong immunoglobulin gene enhancers, leading to constitutive overexpression of MYC protein. MYC is a transcription factor that regulates cell growth, proliferation, apoptosis, and metabolism. Its overexpression drives uncontrolled cell proliferation, characteristic of Burkitt lymphoma.
*   **Morphology:** The characteristic "starry sky" appearance is due to the numerous TBMs (macrophages phagocytosing apoptotic tumor cells) scattered among the rapidly proliferating lymphoma cells. The lymphoma cells themselves are medium-sized, with round nuclei, finely clumped chromatin, prominent nucleoli, and scant cytoplasm, reflecting their high proliferative rate and limited differentiation.
*   **Immunophenotype:** Burkitt lymphoma cells are B-cells (CD20+, CD79a+) that typically express germinal center markers like CD10 and BCL6. They often express MUM1 (a plasma cell marker) and lack BCL2 expression (unlike follicular lymphoma).
*   **Clinical Variants:** Endemic (African), sporadic (non-endemic), and immunodeficiency-associated forms exist. The endemic form is strongly associated with EBV infection and often presents in childhood with jaw involvement.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Diffuse large B-cell lymphoma (DLBCL):** DLBCL is the most common type of non-Hodgkin lymphoma. While it can present with rapid growth and systemic symptoms, the morphology is typically more pleomorphic (variable cell size and shape), and the specific immunophenotype (CD20+, CD10 variable, BCL2 variable, MUM1 variable) and translocation (MYC is not the defining feature, although MYC rearrangements can occur in a subset, often associated with double-hit lymphomas) differ from the classic Burkitt lymphoma described. DLBCL lacks the characteristic "starry sky" pattern and the specific t(8;14) translocation is not typical.
- (B) **Follicular lymphoma:** This is typically an indolent (slow-growing) lymphoma composed of small cleaved cells (centrocytes) and large non-cleaved cells (centroblasts) arranged in a follicular pattern. It is characterized by the t(14;18)(q32;q21) translocation involving the BCL2 gene, leading to BCL2 overexpression and inhibition of apoptosis. The cells are typically BCL2 positive, which is negative in Burkitt lymphoma. The morphology and clinical course are distinct.
- (C) **Mantle cell lymphoma:** This is an aggressive B-cell lymphoma characterized by the t(11;14)(q13;q32) translocation involving the CCND1 (cyclin D1) gene. This leads to cyclin D1 overexpression, promoting cell cycle progression. Mantle cell lymphoma cells typically express CD5 (a T-cell marker aberrantly expressed on these B-cells) and cyclin D1. The morphology and genetic hallmark differ significantly from Burkitt lymphoma.
- (E) **Hodgkin lymphoma:** This is characterized by the presence of Reed-Sternberg cells (large, multinucleated cells) in a background of reactive inflammatory cells. The neoplastic cells are typically CD30+ and CD15+, but usually negative for CD20 (B-cell marker). The morphology, immunophenotype, and genetic features are completely different from Burkitt lymphoma.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature             | Burkitt Lymphoma                                  | DLBCL                                       | Follicular Lymphoma                         | Mantle Cell Lymphoma                       | Hodgkin Lymphoma                             |
| :------------------ | :----------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------- |
| **Morphology**      | Medium cells, "starry sky", high mitotic rate    | Large, pleomorphic cells                    | Small cleaved/large cells, follicular pattern | Small/medium cells, often nodular/diffuse   | Reed-Sternberg cells, inflammatory background |
| **Immunophenotype** | CD20+, CD10+, BCL6+, MUM1+, BCL2-                | CD20+, CD10 variable, BCL2 variable, MUM1 variable | CD20+, CD10+, BCL2+                         | CD5+, CD20+, Cyclin D1+                     | RS cells: CD30+, CD15+, CD20-                |
| **Genetics**        | t(8;14)(MYC/IgH) (most common)                   | Variable, MYC rearrangements possible       | t(14;18)(BCL2/IgH)                          | t(11;14)(CCND1/IgH)                         | None specific                               |
| **Clinical**        | Rapid growth, jaw/abdominal masses, endemic areas | Variable presentation, often nodal           | Indolent, nodal, often widespread           | Aggressive, nodal/extranodal                | Nodal, contiguous spread                     |
| **EBV Association** | Strong (especially endemic variant)               | Weak                                        | Weak                                        | Weak                                        | Weak                                         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Burkitt lymphoma is a high-grade B-cell lymphoma characterized by MYC translocation (most commonly t(8;14)), aggressive clinical course, specific morphology ("starry sky"), and immunophenotype (CD20+, CD10+, BCL2-). Key differentials include other aggressive B-cell lymphomas like DLBCL, and indolent lymphomas like follicular lymphoma, which can be distinguished by morphology, immunophenotype (especially BCL2 expression), and genetic translocations. Remember the classic presentation in endemic areas (jaw mass in children/young adults).

<a id='question-113'></a>

### Question 113: Hematopoietic Pathology: Leukemias and Lymphomas: Focus 03-13 (It is composed of intermediate sized lymphoid cell)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Identification of a specific type of lymphoma based on morphology (intermediate-sized lymphoid cells), immunophenotype (CD20+, CD10+, BCL6+), and clinical presentation (diffuse lymphadenopathy, potential for extranodal involvement). This tests knowledge of Diffuse Large B-cell Lymphoma (DLBCL) subtypes, specifically Germinal Center B-cell like (GCB) DLBCL.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old male presents to the emergency department with rapidly enlarging lymph nodes in his neck and axillae over the past 2 weeks, accompanied by night sweats and unintentional weight loss (10 lbs in one month). He denies fever. Physical examination reveals widespread, firm, non-tender lymphadenopathy. A CT scan of the chest, abdomen, and pelvis shows diffuse lymphadenopathy, splenomegaly, and involvement of the Waldeyer's ring. An excisional lymph node biopsy is performed. Histopathology reveals a diffuse proliferation of large lymphoid cells with prominent nucleoli and a high mitotic rate, interspersed with some smaller lymphocytes and histiocytes. The tumor cells are positive for CD20, CD10, and BCL6 by immunohistochemistry, and negative for CD5 and Cyclin D1. Flow cytometry confirms a monoclonal B-cell population. Given these findings, which of the following is the most likely diagnosis?

(A) Burkitt Lymphoma
(B) Follicular Lymphoma, Grade 3B
(C) Mantle Cell Lymphoma
(D) Diffuse Large B-cell Lymphoma, Germinal Center B-cell like (GCB) subtype
(E) Classical Hodgkin Lymphoma

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 55-year-old male with rapid onset (2 weeks) of widespread lymphadenopathy, B symptoms (night sweats, weight loss). This suggests an aggressive lymphoma.
- **Physical Exam:** Firm, non-tender lymphadenopathy confirms lymphoproliferation.
- **Imaging:** Diffuse lymphadenopathy, splenomegaly, Waldeyer's ring involvement indicates systemic disease.
- **Histopathology:** Diffuse proliferation of *large* lymphoid cells with prominent nucleoli and high mitotic rate points towards a high-grade lymphoma. The presence of intermediate-sized cells alongside large cells is also noted.
- **Immunohistochemistry:** CD20+ confirms B-cell lineage. CD10+ and BCL6+ are characteristic markers for germinal center B-cells. CD5- and Cyclin D1- help rule out other B-cell lymphomas.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Monoclonal B-cell population confirms a neoplastic process.
- **Synthesis:** The combination of aggressive clinical presentation, diffuse proliferation of large B-cells with germinal center markers (CD10+, BCL6+), and lack of other specific markers (CD5-, Cyclin D1-) strongly points towards Diffuse Large B-cell Lymphoma (DLBCL) with a germinal center B-cell-like (GCB) phenotype.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **DLBCL:** The most common type of non-Hodgkin lymphoma. It is characterized by a diffuse growth pattern of large B-cells.
- **Subtypes:** DLBCL is broadly classified into Germinal Center B-cell like (GCB) and Activated B-cell like (ABC) subtypes based on gene expression profiling and immunohistochemistry.
- **GCB DLBCL:** Typically expresses germinal center markers like CD10, BCL6, and BCL2. Often associated with translocations involving BCL6 or BCL2 genes. Generally has a better prognosis than ABC subtype if treated appropriately.
- **ABC DLBCL:** Lacks germinal center markers, often expresses markers of activated B-cells (e.g., CD27, CD30). Frequently associated with mutations in genes involved in NF-κB signaling pathway.
- **Diagnosis:** Requires histopathology showing diffuse large lymphoid cells, immunohistochemistry to determine lineage and subtype, and often flow cytometry or genetic studies.
- **Treatment:** Typically involves R-CHOP chemotherapy (Rituximab, Cyclophosphamide, Doxorubicin, Vincristine, Prednisone).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Burkitt Lymphoma:** This is a highly aggressive B-cell lymphoma, but it is characterized by *small*, uniform, medium-sized lymphoid cells with a "starry sky" appearance (due to numerous tingible body macrophages). It typically expresses CD20 and CD10 but is usually negative for BCL6. It is strongly associated with MYC translocations (e.g., t(8;14)). The large cell morphology and BCL6 positivity in the vignette rule this out.
- (B) **Follicular Lymphoma, Grade 3B:** Follicular lymphoma is typically an indolent lymphoma, although Grade 3B is considered aggressive. It arises from germinal center B-cells and expresses CD10 and BCL2. However, it typically retains a follicular growth pattern (even if disrupted in Grade 3B) and the cells are usually smaller than those seen in DLBCL. While it can express CD10 and BCL2, the diffuse proliferation of *large* cells with high mitotic rate and BCL6 positivity is more characteristic of DLBCL.
- (C) **Mantle Cell Lymphoma:** This is a B-cell lymphoma arising from the mantle zone of lymph follicles. It is characterized by the expression of CD5 (a T-cell marker aberrantly expressed) and Cyclin D1 (due to t(11;14) translocation). The cells are typically small to medium-sized, although a blastoid variant exists. The vignette explicitly states CD5- and Cyclin D1-, ruling out Mantle Cell Lymphoma.
- (E) **Classical Hodgkin Lymphoma:** This is characterized by the presence of Reed-Sternberg cells (large, multinucleated cells) in a background of reactive inflammatory cells (lymphocytes, eosinophils, plasma cells, histiocytes). The Reed-Sternberg cells are typically positive for CD30 and CD15, but negative for CD20 (B-cell marker) and CD10. The vignette describes a diffuse proliferation of large *lymphoid* cells (not Reed-Sternberg cells) that are CD20 positive, ruling out Hodgkin Lymphoma.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature             | DLBCL (GCB)        | Burkitt Lymphoma   | Follicular Lymphoma (Grade 3B) | Mantle Cell Lymphoma | Hodgkin Lymphoma      |
|----------------------|--------------------|--------------------|--------------------------------|----------------------|-----------------------|
| **Cell Size**        | Large              | Medium/Small       | Small/Medium                   | Small/Medium         | Reed-Sternberg (Large) |
| **Growth Pattern**   | Diffuse            | Diffuse            | Follicular (disrupted in 3B)   | Diffuse/Nodular      | Mixed Cellularity     |
| **CD20**             | +                  | +                  | +                              | +                    | - (RS cells)          |
| **CD10**             | +                  | +                  | +                              | -                    | -                    |
| **BCL6**             | +                  | -                  | +/-                            | -                    | -                    |
| **CD5**              | -                  | -                  | -                              | +                    | -                    |
| **Cyclin D1**        | -                  | -                  | -                              | +                    | -                    |
| **BCL2**             | + (often)          | -                  | +                              | -                    | -                    |
| **Mitotic Rate**     | High               | High               | Variable                       | Variable             | Low                  |
| **Clinical Course**  | Aggressive         | Very Aggressive    | Indolent/Aggressive (Grade 3B)  | Indolent/Aggressive  | Variable              |
| **Key Cells**        | Large B-cells      | Small B-cells      | Centrocytes/Centroblasts       | Small B-cells        | Reed-Sternberg cells  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key takeaway is recognizing DLBCL, particularly the GCB subtype, based on the combination of large cell morphology, diffuse growth pattern, and specific immunophenotype (CD20+, CD10+, BCL6+, CD5-, Cyclin D1-). Differentiating it from other aggressive lymphomas like Burkitt lymphoma (small cells, BCL6-) and Mantle Cell Lymphoma (CD5+, Cyclin D1+) is crucial. Follicular lymphoma grade 3B can mimic DLBCL but usually retains some follicular architecture and has distinct genetic features. Hodgkin lymphoma is distinguished by the presence of Reed-Sternberg cells and a different immunophenotype.

<a id='question-114'></a>

### Question 114: Hematopoietic Pathology: Leukemias and Lymphomas: Focus 03-14 (due to numerous reactive tingible body macrophages)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the ability to recognize the characteristic histological features of Hodgkin lymphoma, specifically the presence of Reed-Sternberg cells within a background rich in reactive inflammatory cells, including tingible body macrophages, and differentiate it from other lymphoid neoplasms or reactive processes. (考察霍奇金淋巴瘤的组织学特征，特别是Reed-Sternberg细胞的存在以及背景中丰富的反应性炎症细胞，包括有触丝体巨噬细胞，并将其与其他淋巴瘤或反应性过程区分开来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to his primary care physician with a 3-month history of painless swelling in his left supraclavicular lymph node. He denies fever, night sweats, or weight loss (B symptoms). Physical examination reveals a firm, non-tender, 3 cm lymph node in the left supraclavicular region. No other lymphadenopathy is noted. Complete blood count (CBC) is unremarkable. An excisional biopsy of the affected lymph node is performed. Histopathological examination reveals a lymph node architecture effaced by a mixed inflammatory infiltrate composed of lymphocytes, plasma cells, eosinophils, and numerous large macrophages with pale cytoplasm and prominent nucleoli, often containing cellular debris within their cytoplasm (tingible body macrophages). Scattered throughout this background are large, atypical, binucleated or multinucleated cells with abundant eosinophilic cytoplasm and prominent inclusion-like nucleoli, resembling "owl's eyes". Immunohistochemical staining shows these atypical cells are positive for CD30 and CD15, and negative for CD45 (LCA) and most B-cell and T-cell markers.

Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis?

(A) Diffuse Large B-cell Lymphoma (DLBCL)
(B) Follicular Lymphoma
(C) Classical Hodgkin Lymphoma
(D) Anaplastic Large Cell Lymphoma (ALCL)
(E) Reactive Lymphadenitis

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with painless supraclavicular lymphadenopathy for 3 months, without B symptoms. This presentation is common for lymphoma, particularly Hodgkin lymphoma, which often presents with localized lymphadenopathy.
- **Physical Exam:** Firm, non-tender lymph node. This is typical for neoplastic lymphadenopathy.
- **CBC:** Unremarkable. This is common in early-stage Hodgkin lymphoma, where peripheral blood involvement is less frequent than in many non-Hodgkin lymphomas.
- **Histopathology:**
    - Effaced lymph node architecture: Indicates a neoplastic or severe inflammatory process.
    - Mixed inflammatory infiltrate: Lymphocytes, plasma cells, eosinophils, and *numerous* large macrophages with pale cytoplasm and prominent nucleoli containing cellular debris (tingible body macrophages). This specific inflammatory background is highly characteristic of Hodgkin lymphoma.
    - Large, atypical, binucleated/multinucleated cells with abundant eosinophilic cytoplasm and prominent inclusion-like nucleoli ("owl's eyes"): This is the classic description of Reed-Sternberg (RS) cells, the hallmark cells of Hodgkin lymphoma.
- **Immunohistochemistry:**
    - RS cells positive for CD30 and CD15: These are characteristic markers for RS cells in classical Hodgkin lymphoma.
    - RS cells negative for CD45 (LCA) and most B/T cell markers: This indicates the RS cells are non-lymphoid or have lost expression of these markers, which is typical for classical Hodgkin lymphoma.

These findings collectively point strongly towards Classical Hodgkin Lymphoma. The presence of RS cells within a background rich in reactive inflammatory cells, especially tingible body macrophages, is the defining feature.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Classical Hodgkin Lymphoma (cHL):** Characterized by the presence of malignant Reed-Sternberg (RS) cells (and variants like Hodgkin cells) within a reactive cellular background.
- **RS Cells:** Originate from germinal center B cells, but typically lose expression of most B-cell markers (CD20, CD79a) and CD45 (LCA). They express CD30, CD15, and often MHC class II. Their exact nature and origin are still debated, but they are considered malignant.
- **Reactive Background:** The inflammatory infiltrate (lymphocytes, eosinophils, plasma cells, histiocytes, neutrophils) is crucial for the survival and proliferation of RS cells, which secrete cytokines (e.g., IL-13, IL-5, CCL5) that attract and activate these reactive cells.
- **Tingible Body Macrophages (TBMs):** These are specialized macrophages found in germinal centers that phagocytose apoptotic cells and immune complexes. In cHL, they are abundant and often contain nuclear debris from apoptotic RS cells or other cells, giving them a characteristic appearance. Their presence is a key histological feature supporting the diagnosis of cHL.
- **Pathogenesis:** The malignant RS cells are sparse (often <1% of the cellular infiltrate) and are surrounded by a dense reactive inflammatory response. The RS cells are thought to drive the inflammatory process, which contributes to the clinical symptoms.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Diffuse Large B-cell Lymphoma (DLBCL):** This is a common non-Hodgkin lymphoma. Histologically, it shows a diffuse proliferation of large atypical B lymphocytes, effacing the normal lymph node architecture. While there might be some reactive cells, the characteristic RS cells and the prominent background of tingible body macrophages are absent. DLBCL cells are typically positive for B-cell markers (CD20, CD79a) and CD45.
- (B) **Follicular Lymphoma:** This is another common non-Hodgkin lymphoma, typically indolent. It is characterized by a nodular (follicular) growth pattern, mimicking germinal centers, composed of centrocytes and centroblasts. RS cells and the specific inflammatory background described are not features of follicular lymphoma. Follicular lymphoma cells are positive for B-cell markers (CD20, CD79a, CD10, BCL2) and CD45.
- (D) **Anaplastic Large Cell Lymphoma (ALCL):** This is a type of T-cell lymphoma. Histologically, it features large, pleomorphic cells with horseshoe-shaped nuclei (hallmark cells). These cells are typically positive for CD30 and CD45 (unlike RS cells) and often positive for ALK-1 (in ALK-positive ALCL). The characteristic RS cells and the specific inflammatory background of cHL are absent.
- (E) **Reactive Lymphadenitis:** This is an inflammatory response in the lymph node, often due to infection or autoimmune disease. While it shows an inflammatory infiltrate with macrophages (including tingible body macrophages), it lacks the characteristic malignant RS cells. The architecture may be partially preserved, and the cellular infiltrate is typically polyclonal. The presence of atypical, large, binucleated cells with specific immunophenotype (CD30+, CD15+, CD45-) rules out reactive lymphadenitis.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | Classical Hodgkin Lymphoma (cHL) | DLBCL                     | Follicular Lymphoma          | ALCL                        | Reactive Lymphadenitis       |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------ | :------------------------ | :-------------------------- | :-------------------------- | :-------------------------- |
| **Hallmark Cell**       | Reed-Sternberg (RS) cell        | Large atypical B-cell     | Centrocyte/Centroblast      | Hallmark cell (CD30+, CD45+) | None (polyclonal reactive) |
| **RS Cell Markers**     | CD30+, CD15+, CD45-             | CD20+, CD79a+, CD45+      | CD20+, CD79a+, CD10+, CD45+ | CD30+, CD45+, often ALK+    | N/A                        |
| **Background**          | Rich reactive infiltrate (TBMs) | Diffuse atypical cells    | Nodular, follicular pattern | Diffuse atypical cells      | Polyclonal reactive cells   |
| **Architecture**        | Effaced                         | Effaced                   | Nodular (partially preserved) | Effaced                   | Partially preserved         |
| **Clinical Presentation** | Often localized, B symptoms +/- | Often widespread, B symptoms | Often widespread, indolent   | Variable, B symptoms +/-    | Usually associated w/ infection |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The presence of Reed-Sternberg cells within a background rich in reactive inflammatory cells, particularly tingible body macrophages, is pathognomonic for Classical Hodgkin Lymphoma. Differentiating cHL from other lymphomas (like DLBCL, Follicular Lymphoma, ALCL) and reactive processes requires careful histological and immunohistochemical analysis, focusing on the specific morphology of the atypical cells and their immunophenotype. Remember that RS cells are typically CD30+, CD15+, and CD45-.

<a id='question-115'></a>

### Question 115: Hematopoietic Pathology: Leukemias and Lymphomas: Focus 03-15 (tumor cells). There is a characteristic t(8;14) tr)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the understanding of the characteristic chromosomal translocation t(8;14) associated with Burkitt lymphoma, its resulting oncogene (MYC), and the clinical presentation of this aggressive B-cell lymphoma, particularly in the context of Epstein-Barr virus (EBV) association and jaw involvement.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 7-year-old boy from a rural area in equatorial Africa presents with a rapidly enlarging, painless mass on his mandible (lower jaw). He also reports intermittent fevers, night sweats, and fatigue over the past month. Physical examination reveals a significant swelling of the right side of the jaw, extending into the oral cavity, with associated dental pain. Intraoral examination shows ulceration and bleeding of the gingiva. Vital signs are notable for a temperature of 38.5°C (101.3°F) and a heart rate of 110 bpm. A complete blood count (CBC) shows leukocytosis (WBC 25,000/µL) with a predominance of lymphocytes and blasts, anemia (Hgb 8.5 g/dL), and thrombocytopenia (platelets 50,000/µL). A biopsy of the jaw mass reveals a diffuse proliferation of medium-sized lymphoid cells with round nuclei, finely clumped chromatin, multiple small nucleoli, and scant cytoplasm. These cells are positive for CD20, CD10, and BCL6, and negative for CD5 and BCL2. Cytogenetic analysis of the tumor cells demonstrates a characteristic translocation involving chromosomes 8 and 14.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying molecular mechanism driving the pathogenesis of this patient's condition?

(A) Overexpression of the BCL2 protein due to the t(14;18) translocation, leading to inhibition of apoptosis.
(B) Constitutive activation of the MYC transcription factor due to the t(8;14) translocation, promoting cell proliferation.
(C) Dysregulation of the PAX5 transcription factor due to the t(11;14) translocation, impairing B-cell differentiation.
(D) Activation of the RAS oncogene due to a point mutation, leading to uncontrolled cell growth signaling.
(E) Aberrant expression of the CD30 protein due to a chromosomal deletion, characteristic of Hodgkin lymphoma.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 7-year-old boy from equatorial Africa. This demographic is classic for endemic Burkitt lymphoma, often associated with EBV.
- **Presenting Symptoms:** Rapidly enlarging painless jaw mass, fever, night sweats, fatigue. These are systemic symptoms (B symptoms) and a characteristic location (jaw) for endemic Burkitt lymphoma.
- **Physical Exam:** Jaw swelling, ulceration, bleeding. Consistent with an aggressive tumor invading local tissues.
- **Labs:** Leukocytosis with lymphocytosis/blasts, anemia, thrombocytopenia. Suggests bone marrow involvement or systemic spread of lymphoma, leading to cytopenias.
- **Biopsy:** Diffuse proliferation of medium-sized lymphoid cells with specific morphology (round nuclei, clumped chromatin, small nucleoli, scant cytoplasm) - "starry sky" appearance is often seen but not explicitly mentioned here. Immunophenotype: CD20+, CD10+, BCL6+, CD5-, BCL2-. This profile is highly characteristic of Burkitt lymphoma (a mature B-cell lymphoma).
- **Cytogenetics:** t(8;14) translocation. This is the hallmark genetic abnormality of Burkitt lymphoma.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism driven by the t(8;14) translocation.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- The t(8;14) translocation juxtaposes the *MYC* oncogene (located on chromosome 8q24) with the immunoglobulin heavy chain (IgH) locus (located on chromosome 14q32).
- The IgH locus contains highly active enhancer elements that drive transcription.
- The translocation places *MYC* under the control of these IgH enhancers, leading to constitutive and markedly increased expression of the MYC protein.
- MYC is a transcription factor that regulates the expression of numerous genes involved in cell cycle progression, proliferation, apoptosis, and metabolism.
- Overexpression of MYC drives uncontrolled cell proliferation, which is the hallmark of Burkitt lymphoma, an aggressive B-cell malignancy.
- The clinical presentation (rapidly growing jaw mass in a child from endemic area), morphology, immunophenotype (CD20+, CD10+, BCL6+, BCL2-), and cytogenetics (t(8;14)) all point definitively to Burkitt lymphoma. The question directly links the cytogenetic finding to the underlying molecular mechanism.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Overexpression of BCL2 protein due to the t(14;18) translocation is characteristic of Follicular Lymphoma, not Burkitt lymphoma. The t(14;18) involves the *BCL2* gene on chromosome 18 and the IgH locus on chromosome 14, leading to BCL2 overexpression and inhibition of apoptosis. The immunophenotype in the vignette (BCL2-) argues against this.
- (C): Dysregulation of the PAX5 transcription factor due to the t(11;14) translocation is characteristic of Mantle Cell Lymphoma. The t(11;14) involves the *CCND1* (cyclin D1) gene on chromosome 11 and the IgH locus on chromosome 14, leading to cyclin D1 overexpression and cell cycle dysregulation. PAX5 is a B-cell specific transcription factor, but its dysregulation is not the primary mechanism driven by t(11;14) in Mantle Cell Lymphoma.
- (D): Activation of the RAS oncogene (e.g., KRAS, NRAS, HRAS) due to point mutations is common in various cancers, including some leukemias (e.g., AML) and solid tumors, but it is not the characteristic mechanism associated with the t(8;14) translocation in Burkitt lymphoma.
- (E): Aberrant expression of the CD30 protein is characteristic of Hodgkin lymphoma (specifically Reed-Sternberg cells) and Anaplastic Large Cell Lymphoma (ALCL). Chromosomal deletions are not the primary mechanism for CD30 expression in these lymphomas; rather, it's often due to transcriptional dysregulation or other genetic events. This is unrelated to the t(8;14) translocation.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Burkitt Lymphoma (t(8;14)) | Follicular Lymphoma (t(14;18)) | Mantle Cell Lymphoma (t(11;14)) | Hodgkin Lymphoma (CD30+) |
|----------------------|-----------------------------|--------------------------------|---------------------------------|--------------------------|
| **Translocation**   | t(8;14)                     | t(14;18)                       | t(11;14)                        | N/A                      |
| **Gene Involved**   | *MYC*                       | *BCL2*                         | *CCND1*                         | N/A                      |
| **Mechanism**       | MYC overexpression          | BCL2 overexpression            | Cyclin D1 overexpression        | CD30 expression          |
| **Cell Type**       | Mature B-cell               | Mature B-cell                  | Mature B-cell                   | Reed-Sternberg cells     |
| **Immunophenotype** | CD20+, CD10+, BCL6+, BCL2-   | CD20+, CD10+, BCL6+, BCL2+      | CD20+, CD5+, Cyclin D1+         | CD30+, CD15+             |
| **Clinical**        | Jaw mass, endemic Africa     | Nodal, indolent                 | Nodal, intermediate              | Nodal, B symptoms        |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The t(8;14) translocation is pathognomonic for Burkitt lymphoma, leading to MYC overexpression and uncontrolled proliferation. Differentiating Burkitt lymphoma from other B-cell lymphomas often involves recognizing characteristic translocations (t(14;18) for Follicular Lymphoma, t(11;14) for Mantle Cell Lymphoma) and immunophenotypes (BCL2 expression). Remember that MYC overexpression is the key consequence of t(8;14).

<a id='question-116'></a>

### Question 116: Hematopoietic Pathology: Leukemias and Lymphomas: Focus 03-16 (immunoglobulin heavy chain locus in most cases.)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the genetic basis of Burkitt lymphoma, specifically the characteristic chromosomal translocation involving the immunoglobulin heavy chain locus (IGH) and the MYC oncogene, leading to MYC overexpression and uncontrolled cell proliferation. (本题考察伯基特淋巴瘤的遗传学基础，特别是涉及免疫球蛋白重链基因座 (IGH) 和 MYC 癌基因的特征性染色体易位，导致 MYC 过度表达和细胞不受控制的增殖。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 12-year-old boy from a rural area in equatorial Africa presents with a rapidly enlarging mass in his jaw, causing facial asymmetry and difficulty chewing. He also reports intermittent fevers, night sweats, and weight loss over the past month. Physical examination reveals a firm, non-tender mass involving the mandible, along with generalized lymphadenopathy, particularly in the cervical and inguinal regions. Laboratory studies show elevated lactate dehydrogenase (LDH) levels (1800 U/L, normal <250 U/L) and hyperuricemia (10 mg/dL, normal <7 mg/dL). A biopsy of the jaw mass shows a diffuse proliferation of medium-sized lymphoid cells with round nuclei, finely dispersed chromatin, multiple small nucleoli, and scant cytoplasm. These cells are positive for CD20 and CD10, and negative for CD5 and BCL2. Cytogenetic analysis reveals a translocation between chromosomes 8 and 14, t(8;14)(q24;q32).

Which of the following genetic alterations is most likely responsible for the pathogenesis of this patient's condition?

(A) A translocation involving the BCL2 gene on chromosome 18 and the immunoglobulin heavy chain locus on chromosome 14, leading to BCL2 overexpression.
(B) A translocation involving the MYC gene on chromosome 8 and the immunoglobulin light chain kappa locus on chromosome 2, leading to MYC overexpression.
(C) A translocation involving the MYC gene on chromosome 8 and the immunoglobulin heavy chain locus on chromosome 14, leading to MYC overexpression.
(D) A translocation involving the PAX5 gene on chromosome 9 and the immunoglobulin heavy chain locus on chromosome 14, leading to PAX5 overexpression.
(E) A translocation involving the BCL6 gene on chromosome 17 and the immunoglobulin heavy chain locus on chromosome 14, leading to BCL6 overexpression.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 12-year-old boy from equatorial Africa. This demographic is classic for endemic Burkitt lymphoma.
- **Presenting Symptoms:** Rapidly enlarging jaw mass (common site for endemic Burkitt), facial asymmetry, difficulty chewing, intermittent fevers, night sweats, weight loss (B symptoms).
- **Physical Exam:** Firm jaw mass, generalized lymphadenopathy.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Elevated LDH (indicates high cell turnover/tumor burden), hyperuricemia (due to rapid cell lysis).
- **Histopathology:** Diffuse proliferation of medium-sized lymphoid cells with characteristic "starry sky" appearance (implied by the description: round nuclei, finely dispersed chromatin, multiple small nucleoli, scant cytoplasm). Immunophenotype: CD20+ (B-cell marker), CD10+ (germinal center B-cell marker), CD5- (rules out mantle cell lymphoma), BCL2- (rules out follicular lymphoma).
- **Cytogenetics:** t(8;14)(q24;q32). This is the hallmark translocation for Burkitt lymphoma.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the genetic alteration responsible for the pathogenesis.

The combination of clinical presentation (endemic Burkitt lymphoma in a child from Africa, jaw mass, B symptoms), histopathology (medium-sized B-cells, CD20+, CD10+, BCL2-), and the specific cytogenetic finding t(8;14)(q24;q32) strongly points to Burkitt lymphoma. The question asks for the consequence of this translocation. The translocation places the MYC oncogene (located at 8q24) under the control of the immunoglobulin heavy chain (IGH) locus promoter/enhancer (located at 14q32). This leads to constitutive overexpression of MYC, driving uncontrolled B-cell proliferation.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Burkitt Lymphoma Pathogenesis:** Burkitt lymphoma is a highly aggressive B-cell non-Hodgkin lymphoma. Its pathogenesis is driven by the dysregulation of the MYC oncogene.
- **MYC Gene:** MYC is a transcription factor that regulates cell growth, proliferation, differentiation, and apoptosis. Its activity is tightly controlled in normal cells.
- **Chromosomal Translocations:** In Burkitt lymphoma, MYC is typically involved in chromosomal translocations that place it near strong immunoglobulin gene loci.
- **t(8;14)(q24;q32):** This is the most common translocation (~80% of cases), involving the MYC gene on chromosome 8 and the IGH locus on chromosome 14. The IGH locus contains powerful enhancers that drive high levels of MYC transcription, leading to MYC overexpression.
- **Other Translocations:** Less common translocations involve MYC and the immunoglobulin light chain loci: t(2;8)(p12;q24) (MYC and IGL kappa locus) or t(8;22)(q24;q11) (MYC and IGL lambda locus). All these translocations result in MYC overexpression.
- **Molecular Mechanism:** The translocation juxtaposes the MYC gene with the immunoglobulin locus, placing the MYC promoter under the control of immunoglobulin enhancers. This leads to aberrant and constitutive expression of the MYC protein, promoting cell cycle entry and inhibiting differentiation, ultimately leading to lymphoma development.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): A translocation involving the BCL2 gene on chromosome 18 and the immunoglobulin heavy chain locus on chromosome 14, t(14;18)(q32;q21), is characteristic of follicular lymphoma. This translocation leads to overexpression of the anti-apoptotic protein BCL2, promoting cell survival. The clinical presentation (slow-growing lymphadenopathy) and immunophenotype (CD5-, CD10+, BCL2+) are different from the case described.
- (B): A translocation involving the MYC gene on chromosome 8 and the immunoglobulin light chain kappa locus on chromosome 2, t(2;8)(p12;q24), is another variant translocation seen in Burkitt lymphoma, but it is less common than t(8;14). While it does lead to MYC overexpression, t(8;14) is the most frequent and the one specifically identified in the vignette.
- (D): A translocation involving the PAX5 gene on chromosome 9 and the immunoglobulin heavy chain locus on chromosome 14 is not a recognized translocation associated with Burkitt lymphoma or other common lymphomas. PAX5 is a B-cell specific transcription factor, but its dysregulation is not the primary driver in this case.
- (E): A translocation involving the BCL6 gene on chromosome 17 and the immunoglobulin heavy chain locus on chromosome 14, t(14;17)(q32;q21), is characteristic of diffuse large B-cell lymphoma (DLBCL), particularly the germinal center B-cell-like subtype. This translocation leads to BCL6 overexpression, which represses B-cell differentiation. The clinical presentation and immunophenotype (often CD10+, BCL2+, BCL6+) differ from the case described.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Burkitt Lymphoma (Endemic) | Follicular Lymphoma | Diffuse Large B-cell Lymphoma (DLBCL) | Mantle Cell Lymphoma |
|----------------------|---------------------------|----------------------|--------------------------------------|----------------------|
| **Typical Translocation** | t(8;14)(MYC/IGH)         | t(14;18)(IGH/BCL2)   | t(14;17)(IGH/BCL6) or t(11;14)(CCND1/IGH) | t(11;14)(CCND1/IGH)   |
| **Gene Involved**     | MYC                      | BCL2                 | BCL6, CCND1                           | CCND1                |
| **Partner Locus**     | IGH                      | IGH                  | IGH                                  | IGH                  |
| **Immunophenotype**   | CD20+, CD10+, BCL2-       | CD20+, CD10+, BCL2+   | CD20+, CD10+/- BCL2+/- BCL6+        | CD20+, CD5+, BCL2+/-   |
| **Clinical Presentation**| Jaw mass, rapid growth   | Slow lymphadenopathy | Variable, often bulky mass          | Lymphadenopathy, splenomegaly |
| **LDH**               | High                      | Normal/Mildly Elevated| Variable                              | Variable             |
| **Age**               | Children/Young Adults     | Adults               | Adults                               | Middle-aged/Elderly  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Burkitt lymphoma is characterized by the t(8;14) translocation leading to MYC overexpression. Remember to differentiate it from follicular lymphoma (t(14;18), BCL2 overexpression) and DLBCL (often t(14;17) or t(11;14), BCL6 or CCND1 overexpression) based on clinical presentation, histology, immunophenotype, and cytogenetics. The endemic form often presents as a jaw mass in children from equatorial Africa.

<a id='question-117'></a>

### Question 117: Hematopoietic Pathology: Leukemias and Lymphomas: Focus 03-17 (• African type: endemic form)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of Burkitt lymphoma, specifically the endemic (African) variant, its association with Epstein-Barr virus (EBV), characteristic cytogenetic translocation (t(8;14)), and its typical clinical presentation in endemic regions. (本题考察伯基特淋巴瘤，特别是内源性（非洲型）变体，其与EBV的关系，特征性细胞遗传学易位（t(8;14)），以及在内源性地区典型的临床表现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 7-year-old boy living in a rural village in equatorial Africa presents with a rapidly enlarging mass in his jaw, causing facial asymmetry and difficulty chewing. He also reports intermittent fevers and night sweats for the past month. Physical examination reveals a firm, non-tender mass involving the mandible, along with generalized lymphadenopathy, particularly in the cervical and inguinal regions. A complete blood count shows mild anemia (Hb 9.5 g/dL, normal range 11.5-15.5 g/dL), leukocytosis (WBC 18,000/µL, normal range 4,500-11,000/µL) with a predominance of lymphocytes, and thrombocytopenia (platelets 80,000/µL, normal range 150,000-450,000/µL). A biopsy of the jaw mass shows a diffuse proliferation of medium-sized lymphoid cells with round nuclei, finely clumped chromatin, multiple small nucleoli, and scant cytoplasm. The cells are negative for CD20 but positive for CD10 and BCL6. Cytogenetic analysis reveals a translocation between chromosomes 8 and 14, t(8;14)(q24;q32). Which of the following is the most likely underlying molecular mechanism driving the pathogenesis of this patient's condition?

(A) Fusion of the *MYC* oncogene on chromosome 8 with the immunoglobulin heavy chain (IgH) locus on chromosome 14, leading to constitutive *MYC* overexpression.
(B) Fusion of the *BCL2* oncogene on chromosome 18 with the immunoglobulin heavy chain (IgH) locus on chromosome 14, resulting in inhibition of apoptosis.
(C) Activation of the *RAS* oncogene due to a point mutation, leading to constitutive activation of downstream signaling pathways involved in cell proliferation.
(D) Insertion of viral DNA encoding the *EBNA* protein into the host cell genome, leading to uncontrolled cell growth and proliferation.
(E) Deletion of tumor suppressor genes on chromosome 13, such as *RB1* and *TP13*, resulting in loss of cell cycle control.

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 7-year-old boy from equatorial Africa. This geographic location is a key clue pointing towards endemic Burkitt lymphoma.
*   **Presenting Symptoms:** Rapidly enlarging jaw mass (common site for endemic Burkitt lymphoma), facial asymmetry, difficulty chewing, intermittent fevers, night sweats. These symptoms suggest a rapidly growing malignancy.
*   **Physical Exam:** Firm, non-tender jaw mass, generalized lymphadenopathy. Consistent with lymphoma.
*   **Laboratory Findings:** Mild anemia, leukocytosis (lymphocytic predominance), thrombocytopenia. Suggests bone marrow involvement or systemic disease affecting hematopoiesis.
*   **Histopathology:** Diffuse proliferation of medium-sized lymphoid cells with characteristic morphology (round nuclei, clumped chromatin, small nucleoli, scant cytoplasm). This is typical for Burkitt lymphoma.
*   **Immunophenotype:** CD20 negative, CD10 positive, BCL6 positive. This immunophenotype is characteristic of Burkitt lymphoma (a B-cell lymphoma, but often CD20 negative or dim).
*   **Cytogenetics:** t(8;14)(q24;q32). This is the hallmark cytogenetic abnormality associated with Burkitt lymphoma.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying molecular mechanism.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   The patient presents with classic features of endemic Burkitt lymphoma: young age, African origin, jaw mass, characteristic histology (starry sky appearance, although not explicitly mentioned, the cell description fits), and immunophenotype.
*   The cytogenetic finding of t(8;14)(q24;q32) is pathognomonic for Burkitt lymphoma.
*   This translocation juxtaposes the *MYC* proto-oncogene (located on chromosome 8q24) with the immunoglobulin heavy chain (IgH) locus (located on chromosome 14q32).
*   The IgH locus is highly active in B-cells, undergoing significant transcription.
*   The translocation places the *MYC* gene under the control of the strong IgH enhancer elements.
*   This leads to constitutive, high-level overexpression of the MYC protein, a transcription factor that regulates cell cycle progression, proliferation, apoptosis, and metabolism.
*   Sustained MYC overexpression drives uncontrolled cell proliferation, characteristic of Burkitt lymphoma.
*   EBV infection is strongly associated with endemic Burkitt lymphoma (present in ~95% of cases), but the *MYC* translocation is the direct molecular driver of the malignancy. EBV likely contributes by promoting B-cell proliferation and immortalization, increasing the risk of the *MYC* translocation occurring.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Correct.** This accurately describes the molecular consequence of the t(8;14) translocation in Burkitt lymphoma: fusion of *MYC* with IgH enhancers leading to *MYC* overexpression.
- (B): Fusion of *BCL2* with IgH (t(14;18)) is characteristic of Follicular Lymphoma, leading to inhibition of apoptosis. While BCL2 is involved in apoptosis regulation, it's not the primary driver in Burkitt lymphoma, and the translocation is different.
- (C): *RAS* mutations are common in many cancers, including some leukemias and lymphomas, but they are not the specific hallmark mechanism of Burkitt lymphoma. The defining feature is the *MYC* translocation.
- (D): While EBV is strongly associated with endemic Burkitt lymphoma, the *EBNA* proteins encoded by EBV contribute to B-cell immortalization and proliferation, but the direct oncogenic driver is the *MYC* translocation, not the viral DNA insertion itself. The question asks for the *underlying molecular mechanism driving the pathogenesis*, which is the *MYC* deregulation caused by the translocation.
- (E): Deletions of *RB1* and *TP13* on chromosome 13 are characteristic of Retinoblastoma, not Burkitt lymphoma. While loss of tumor suppressor function contributes to cancer, this specific deletion is not the defining mechanism for Burkitt lymphoma.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Burkitt Lymphoma (Endemic) | Follicular Lymphoma | Retinoblastoma |
| :------------------ | :------------------------ | :------------------ | :------------- |
| **Typical Age**     | Children/Young Adults     | Adults              | Children       |
| **Geography**       | Equatorial Africa         | Worldwide           | Worldwide      |
| **Primary Site**    | Jaw, Abdomen              | Lymph Nodes         | Eye            |
| **Cytogenetics**    | t(8;14)                   | t(14;18)            | del(13q)       |
| **Molecular Driver** | *MYC* overexpression      | *BCL2* overexpression | *RB1* loss     |
| **EBV Association** | High (~95%)               | Low                 | None           |
| **Histology**       | Starry Sky, Medium Cells  | Follicular Pattern  | Small Blue Cells |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Burkitt lymphoma, especially the endemic African type, is characterized by the t(8;14) translocation leading to *MYC* overexpression. Remember to differentiate it from Follicular Lymphoma (t(14;18), *BCL2* overexpression) and other lymphomas based on cytogenetics, molecular drivers, and clinical presentation. EBV association is high in endemic Burkitt lymphoma but is not the direct oncogenic mechanism.
