<a id='question-137'></a>

### Question 137: Hematopoietic Pathology: Leukemias and Lymphomas: Focus 03-37
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiating between various types of lymphoma based on morphology, immunophenotype, and genetic features, specifically focusing on the characteristic features of Burkitt lymphoma versus other high-grade B-cell lymphomas. (区分不同类型的淋巴瘤，基于形态学、免疫表型和遗传学特征，特别是伯基特淋巴瘤与其他高级别B细胞淋巴瘤的特征。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 28-year-old male from a rural area in equatorial Africa presents with a rapidly enlarging abdominal mass and complaints of abdominal pain and distension over the past 2 weeks. He also reports intermittent fever and night sweats. Physical examination reveals a large, palpable mass in the lower abdomen, extending to the midline. Laboratory studies show elevated lactate dehydrogenase (LDH) levels (1800 U/L, normal <250 U/L) and hyperuricemia (serum uric acid 10 mg/dL, normal <7 mg/dL). A CT scan of the abdomen and pelvis confirms a large, heterogeneous mass involving the small bowel and mesentery, with associated ascites. A biopsy of the abdominal mass is performed. Histopathology reveals a diffuse proliferation of medium-sized lymphoid cells with round nuclei, finely clumped chromatin, multiple small nucleoli, and scant cytoplasm. The cells are arranged in sheets with interspersed macrophages, some containing phagocytosed cellular debris ("starry sky" appearance). Immunohistochemistry shows the tumor cells positive for CD20, CD10, BCL6, and MUM1, but negative for BCL2 and CD30. Cytogenetic analysis reveals a translocation involving the MYC gene on chromosome 8 and the immunoglobulin heavy chain locus on chromosome 14, t(8;14)(q24;q32).

Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis?

(A) Diffuse Large B-cell Lymphoma (DLBCL)
(B) Follicular Lymphoma
(C) Burkitt Lymphoma
(D) Mantle Cell Lymphoma
(E) Hodgkin Lymphoma

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** Young adult male from equatorial Africa. This geographic location is endemic for certain lymphomas, notably Burkitt lymphoma associated with Epstein-Barr virus (EBV).
*   **Clinical Presentation:** Rapidly enlarging abdominal mass, abdominal pain, fever, night sweats. This suggests a high-grade lymphoma with rapid proliferation and systemic symptoms (B symptoms). The location (abdomen, small bowel, mesentery) is common for Burkitt lymphoma.
*   **Laboratory Findings:** Markedly elevated LDH and hyperuricemia. High LDH is characteristic of high-grade lymphomas due to rapid cell turnover and tumor lysis. Hyperuricemia results from massive cell turnover and release of nucleic acids, which are metabolized to uric acid. This is particularly prominent in Burkitt lymphoma.
*   **Histopathology:** Diffuse proliferation of medium-sized lymphoid cells, round nuclei, clumped chromatin, small nucleoli, scant cytoplasm, "starry sky" appearance (macrophages phagocytosing apoptotic cells). This morphology is highly suggestive of Burkitt lymphoma.
*   **Immunohistochemistry:** CD20+, CD10+, BCL6+, MUM1+, BCL2-, CD30-. This immunophenotype is characteristic of Burkitt lymphoma. CD10 and BCL6 are typically positive, while BCL2 is negative (unlike follicular lymphoma) and CD30 is negative (unlike Hodgkin lymphoma and some DLBCL subtypes). MUM1 positivity is also common in Burkitt lymphoma.
*   **Cytogenetics:** t(8;14)(q24;q32). This translocation juxtaposes the MYC oncogene on chromosome 8 with the immunoglobulin heavy chain locus on chromosome 14, leading to MYC overexpression, which is the defining genetic hallmark of Burkitt lymphoma.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Burkitt Lymphoma Pathogenesis:** Burkitt lymphoma is a highly aggressive B-cell non-Hodgkin lymphoma characterized by a specific chromosomal translocation involving the MYC gene (usually t(8;14), less commonly t(2;8) or t(8;22)). This translocation leads to constitutive overexpression of the MYC protein, a transcription factor that drives cell proliferation and metabolism. The rapid proliferation rate explains the high LDH levels and risk of tumor lysis syndrome (manifesting as hyperuricemia, hyperkalemia, hyperphosphatemia, and hypocalcemia). The "starry sky" appearance reflects the high rate of apoptosis and phagocytosis by macrophages. The immunophenotype (CD20+, CD10+, BCL6+, BCL2-) and the specific MYC translocation are diagnostic hallmarks. The association with EBV is strong, particularly in endemic forms.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Diffuse Large B-cell Lymphoma (DLBCL):** DLBCL is the most common type of non-Hodgkin lymphoma. While it can present rapidly and with high LDH, the morphology is typically more pleomorphic (large cells, prominent nucleoli), the immunophenotype is variable (CD20+, often BCL2+, CD10 variable, BCL6 variable, MUM1 variable), and the characteristic MYC translocation t(8;14) is absent. DLBCL is generally CD30 negative, but some subtypes can be positive. The "starry sky" pattern is less common and less pronounced than in Burkitt lymphoma.
*   **(B) Follicular Lymphoma:** Follicular lymphoma is typically an indolent (slow-growing) lymphoma. It usually presents with generalized lymphadenopathy rather than a rapidly enlarging abdominal mass. Histologically, it shows a follicular growth pattern (nodular aggregates of small cleaved cells and centrocytes). The immunophenotype is typically CD20+, CD10+, BCL6+, BCL2+ (due to t(14;18) involving BCL2), and MUM1-. The MYC translocation is absent. High LDH and hyperuricemia are uncommon unless transformation to a high-grade lymphoma occurs.
*   **(D) Mantle Cell Lymphoma:** Mantle cell lymphoma is an aggressive B-cell lymphoma. It typically involves lymph nodes, spleen, bone marrow, and GI tract. Histologically, the cells are usually small to medium-sized with irregular nuclei. The characteristic immunophenotype is CD20+, CD5+, Cyclin D1+ (due to t(11;14) involving CCND1/BCL1). The MYC translocation is absent. While it can be aggressive, the specific morphology, immunophenotype, and cytogenetics described in the vignette do not fit mantle cell lymphoma.
*   **(E) Hodgkin Lymphoma:** Hodgkin lymphoma is characterized by the presence of Reed-Sternberg cells (large, multinucleated cells) in a background of reactive inflammatory cells. The immunophenotype of Reed-Sternberg cells is typically CD30+, CD15+, and often PAX5+, but usually negative for CD20 and CD10 (though exceptions exist). The MYC translocation is absent. The morphology described in the vignette (diffuse sheets of medium-sized lymphoid cells) is inconsistent with Hodgkin lymphoma.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature             | Burkitt Lymphoma                                  | DLBCL                                        | Follicular Lymphoma                          | Mantle Cell Lymphoma                        | Hodgkin Lymphoma                               |
| :------------------ | :------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- |
| **Clinical**        | Rapid growth, endemic areas, abdominal mass, TLS | Variable, often nodal or extranodal          | Indolent, nodal, generalized lymphadenopathy | Aggressive, nodal/extranodal, GI involvement | Nodal, B symptoms, Reed-Sternberg cells       |
| **Morphology**      | Medium cells, round nuclei, scant cytoplasm, "starry sky" | Large cells, pleomorphic, prominent nucleoli | Follicular pattern, small cleaved cells      | Small/medium, irregular nuclei                | Reed-Sternberg cells, inflammatory background |
| **Immunophenotype** | CD20+, CD10+, BCL6+, MUM1+, BCL2-, CD30-          | CD20+, variable CD10/BCL6/MUM1, variable BCL2 | CD20+, CD10+, BCL6+, BCL2+, MUM1-             | CD20+, CD5+, Cyclin D1+                      | RS cells: CD30+, CD15+, PAX5+, CD20-, CD10-   |
| **Genetics**        | t(8;14), MYC overexpression                       | Variable                                     | t(14;18), BCL2 overexpression                 | t(11;14), CCND1/BCL1 overexpression           | None specific for RS cells                    |
| **LDH/Uric Acid**   | Markedly elevated                                 | Often elevated                               | Usually normal                               | Often elevated                               | Variable                                       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Burkitt lymphoma is a high-grade B-cell lymphoma characterized by rapid proliferation, specific morphology ("starry sky"), immunophenotype (CD10+, BCL6+, MUM1+, BCL2-), and the hallmark t(8;14) translocation leading to MYC overexpression. It often presents with a rapidly enlarging mass, high LDH, and hyperuricemia, particularly in endemic regions. Key differentials include other high-grade B-cell lymphomas like DLBCL (lacks specific morphology, immunophenotype, and MYC translocation) and indolent lymphomas like follicular lymphoma (different morphology, immunophenotype, slower growth).

<a id='question-138'></a>

### Question 138: Hematopoietic Pathology: Leukemias and Lymphomas: Focus 03-38 (associated lymphoid tissue (MALTomas). The lesion )
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the understanding of Mucosa-Associated Lymphoid Tissue (MALT) lymphoma, specifically its pathogenesis, characteristic morphology, immunophenotype, and association with chronic inflammation and *Helicobacter pylori* infection. (考察黏膜相关淋巴组织 (MALT) 淋巴瘤的病理发生机制、形态学特征、免疫表型以及与慢性炎症和幽门螺杆菌感染的关联。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 58-year-old male presents to his primary care physician complaining of chronic dyspepsia and intermittent abdominal pain for the past 5 years. He has a history of peptic ulcer disease, treated unsuccessfully with multiple courses of antibiotics. An upper endoscopy reveals chronic gastritis with prominent lymphoid infiltration in the gastric mucosa. Biopsies are taken. Histopathological examination shows a diffuse proliferation of small to medium-sized atypical lymphocytes infiltrating the gastric glands and lamina propria, disrupting the normal gastric architecture. These lymphocytes are monomorphic, with round nuclei, clumped chromatin, and scant cytoplasm. Immunohistochemical staining reveals the neoplastic cells are positive for CD20, CD79a, and Bcl-2, and negative for CD10 and cyclin D1. Cytogenetic analysis shows a translocation t(11;18)(q21;q21). The patient is subsequently treated with eradication therapy for *Helicobacter pylori*.

Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis?

(A) Follicular lymphoma
(B) Diffuse large B-cell lymphoma (DLBCL)
(C) Gastric MALT lymphoma
(D) Mantle cell lymphoma
(E) Burkitt lymphoma

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 58-year-old male with chronic dyspepsia and abdominal pain, history of peptic ulcer disease, unsuccessful antibiotic treatment. This points towards chronic gastric inflammation, potentially related to *H. pylori*.
*   **Endoscopy Findings:** Chronic gastritis with prominent lymphoid infiltration in the gastric mucosa. This suggests a reactive process or a lymphoproliferative disorder involving the gastric mucosa.
*   **Histopathology:** Diffuse proliferation of small to medium-sized atypical lymphocytes infiltrating gastric glands and lamina propria, disrupting architecture. Monomorphic cells with round nuclei, clumped chromatin, scant cytoplasm. This morphology is characteristic of low-grade B-cell lymphoma.
*   **Immunohistochemistry:** CD20+, CD79a+, Bcl-2+, CD10-, cyclin D1-. This immunophenotype is consistent with a B-cell lymphoma. Bcl-2 positivity is common in MALT lymphoma and follicular lymphoma. CD10 negativity helps differentiate from follicular lymphoma. Cyclin D1 negativity rules out mantle cell lymphoma.
*   **Cytogenetics:** t(11;18)(q21;q21). This translocation fuses the *API2* gene (encoding BCL10) on chromosome 11 with the *IGH* gene on chromosome 14 (note: the question incorrectly states chromosome 18, but the translocation t(11;18) is characteristic of MALT lymphoma, involving *API2* and *MALT1*). This translocation is the hallmark genetic abnormality of MALT lymphoma.
*   **Treatment Context:** Eradication therapy for *H. pylori*. MALT lymphoma is strongly associated with chronic *H. pylori* infection, and eradication of the bacteria can lead to regression of the lymphoma in many cases.
*   **Synthesis:** The combination of chronic gastric inflammation (likely *H. pylori* related), lymphoid infiltration, low-grade B-cell morphology, characteristic immunophenotype (CD20+, CD79a+, Bcl-2+, CD10-, cyclin D1-), the specific translocation t(11;18), and association with *H. pylori* eradication strongly points to Gastric MALT lymphoma.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **MALT Lymphoma Pathogenesis:** MALT lymphoma arises in extranodal sites, most commonly the stomach, often in the setting of chronic inflammation, particularly *H. pylori* gastritis. Chronic antigenic stimulation by *H. pylori* leads to the accumulation of B-cells in the mucosa, forming lymphoid follicles. Over time, these B-cells can acquire genetic alterations, most notably the t(11;18)(q21;q21) translocation, which involves the *MALT1* gene (on chromosome 18) and the immunoglobulin heavy chain locus (*IGH*) (on chromosome 14). The *MALT1* gene encodes a protein involved in NF-κB signaling, which promotes B-cell survival and proliferation. The resulting fusion protein is constitutively active, driving lymphomagenesis. Eradication of *H. pylori* removes the chronic antigenic stimulus, leading to apoptosis of the neoplastic B-cells and often lymphoma regression.
*   **Morphology:** Typically composed of small to medium-sized lymphocytes with round nuclei, clumped chromatin, and scant cytoplasm. Often infiltrates glands and lamina propria in a diffuse pattern, unlike the follicular pattern seen in follicular lymphoma.
*   **Immunophenotype:** B-cell lineage (CD19+, CD20+, CD79a+). Usually positive for Bcl-2. Negative for CD10 (unlike follicular lymphoma) and cyclin D1 (unlike mantle cell lymphoma).
*   **Genetics:** The hallmark translocation is t(11;18)(q21;q21), resulting in the *API2-MALT1* fusion gene.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Follicular lymphoma:** This is a common low-grade B-cell lymphoma, often involving lymph nodes but can occur extranodally. It typically shows a follicular growth pattern (though can be diffuse), is CD10 positive, Bcl-2 positive, and lacks the t(11;18) translocation. The morphology described (diffuse infiltration, monomorphic cells) and the CD10 negativity make this less likely.
*   **(B) Diffuse large B-cell lymphoma (DLBCL):** This is an aggressive high-grade B-cell lymphoma. It typically presents as a rapidly enlarging mass. Histologically, it shows large cells with prominent nucleoli and high mitotic activity. The morphology described (small to medium-sized, monomorphic lymphocytes) is inconsistent with DLBCL. While DLBCL can arise de novo or transform from low-grade lymphomas, the presentation here is classic for a low-grade process.
*   **(D) Mantle cell lymphoma:** This is a B-cell lymphoma typically involving lymph nodes, spleen, and bone marrow. It is characterized by the t(11;14)(q13;q32) translocation involving the *CCND1* gene (cyclin D1), leading to cyclin D1 overexpression. Immunophenotypically, it is CD5 positive and cyclin D1 positive. The immunophenotype (CD10-, cyclin D1-) and translocation (t(11;18), not t(11;14)) rule this out.
*   **(E) Burkitt lymphoma:** This is a highly aggressive B-cell lymphoma characterized by a specific morphology (uniform medium-sized cells with round nuclei, multiple nucleoli, basophilic cytoplasm, often with lipid vacuoles) and immunophenotype (CD10+, Bcl-6+, c-Myc positive). It is associated with translocations involving the *MYC* gene (typically t(8;14)). The morphology, immunophenotype, and genetics described in the vignette are inconsistent with Burkitt lymphoma.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature             | Gastric MALT Lymphoma | Follicular Lymphoma | DLBCL                 | Mantle Cell Lymphoma | Burkitt Lymphoma |
| :------------------ | :-------------------- | :------------------ | :-------------------- | :------------------- | :--------------- |
| **Location**        | Extranodal (Gastric)  | Nodal/Extranodal    | Nodal/Extranodal      | Nodal/Extranodal     | Nodal/Extranodal |
| **Morphology**      | Small/Med Lymphocytes | Follicular/Diffuse  | Large Cells, High Grade | Small/Med Lymphocytes | Medium Cells, High Grade |
| **CD10**            | Negative              | Positive            | Variable              | Negative             | Positive         |
| **Cyclin D1**       | Negative              | Negative            | Negative              | Positive             | Negative         |
| **Bcl-2**           | Positive              | Positive            | Variable              | Positive             | Negative         |
| **t(11;18)**        | Positive              | Negative            | Negative              | Negative             | Negative         |
| **t(11;14)**        | Negative              | Negative            | Negative              | Positive             | Negative         |
| **t(8;14)**         | Negative              | Negative            | Negative              | Negative             | Positive         |
| **H. pylori Assoc.** | Strong                | No                  | No                    | No                   | No               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Gastric MALT lymphoma is a low-grade B-cell lymphoma strongly associated with chronic *H. pylori* infection. It is characterized by the t(11;18) translocation and can regress upon *H. pylori* eradication. Key differentials include other low-grade lymphomas like follicular lymphoma (CD10+, follicular pattern) and higher-grade lymphomas like DLBCL (large cells) and Burkitt lymphoma (specific morphology, t(8;14)). Mantle cell lymphoma is distinguished by cyclin D1 positivity and t(11;14).

<a id='question-139'></a>

### Question 139: Hematopoietic Pathology: Leukemias and Lymphomas: Focus 03-39 (reaction and may be associated with previous autoi)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathogenesis, clinical presentation, and diagnostic features of Extranodal NK/T-cell lymphoma, nasal type, particularly its association with Epstein-Barr virus (EBV) infection and its potential for angioimmunoblastic T-cell lymphoma (AITL) transformation, especially in the context of prior autoimmune disease or immunosuppression. (理解鼻型外周T/NK细胞淋巴瘤的发病机制、临床表现和诊断特征，特别是其与EBV感染的关联以及在先前自身免疫性疾病或免疫抑制背景下向血管内皮细胞淋巴瘤（AITL）转化的可能性。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old woman with a history of systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) treated with long-term azathioprine presents with a 6-month history of progressive nasal obstruction, epistaxis, and facial pain. She also reports recent onset of fever, night sweats, and unintentional weight loss (10 lbs). Physical examination reveals a large, ulcerated mass involving the nasal septum and extending into the maxillary sinus. A biopsy of the nasal mass shows a diffuse proliferation of atypical lymphoid cells infiltrating the nasal mucosa and submucosa, with extensive necrosis and angiocentricity (lymphoid cells surrounding blood vessels). Immunohistochemistry reveals that the tumor cells are positive for CD3, CD56, CD20 (weakly), and CD30, but negative for CD10, CD19, and PAX5. Epstein-Barr virus (EBV) latent membrane protein 1 (LMP1) is strongly positive by in situ hybridization. Given her history of SLE and azathioprine use, and the histological and immunophenotypic findings, what is the most likely diagnosis and associated complication?

(A) Diffuse large B-cell lymphoma (DLBCL), transformed from a pre-existing follicular lymphoma, complicated by EBV reactivation due to immunosuppression.
(B) Burkitt lymphoma, arising de novo in the nasal cavity, associated with c-MYC translocation and EBV infection.
(C) Extranodal NK/T-cell lymphoma, nasal type, associated with EBV infection, potentially transforming into angioimmunoblastic T-cell lymphoma (AITL) due to chronic immunosuppression.
(D) Peripheral T-cell lymphoma, not otherwise specified (PTCL-NOS), arising secondary to chronic inflammation from SLE, characterized by CD3 positivity and lack of specific markers.
(E) Anaplastic large cell lymphoma (ALCL), positive for CD30 and ALK, presenting with nasal mass and systemic symptoms.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old woman with SLE on azathioprine (immunosuppression). This history is crucial as it predisposes to certain lymphomas, particularly EBV-associated ones, and increases the risk of transformation or secondary malignancies.
*   **Presenting Symptoms:** Nasal obstruction, epistaxis, facial pain (6 months), fever, night sweats, weight loss (B symptoms). These symptoms point towards a destructive process in the nasal cavity and systemic involvement, suggestive of malignancy.
*   **Physical Exam:** Ulcerated mass in the nasal septum extending into the maxillary sinus. This localizes the primary lesion and indicates aggressive local invasion.
*   **Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse proliferation of atypical lymphoid cells, extensive necrosis, angiocentricity. These are characteristic features of aggressive lymphomas, particularly NK/T-cell lymphomas.
*   **Immunohistochemistry:** CD3+, CD56+, CD20(weakly)+, CD30+, CD10-, CD19-, PAX5-. This immunophenotype is highly suggestive of an NK/T-cell lymphoma. CD3 and CD56 positivity indicates T-cell or NK-cell lineage. Weak CD20 positivity can sometimes be seen in NK/T-cell lymphomas. Absence of B-cell markers (CD19, CD20, PAX5) rules out B-cell lymphomas.
*   **EBV Status:** LMP1 strongly positive by in situ hybridization. EBV is strongly associated with extranodal NK/T-cell lymphoma, nasal type.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the most likely diagnosis and associated complication, considering the patient's history and findings. The history of SLE and azathioprine use raises suspicion for transformation or secondary malignancy, particularly AITL, which can arise in the setting of chronic immunosuppression or autoimmune disease, sometimes from a precursor NK/T-cell lymphoma.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Extranodal NK/T-cell lymphoma, nasal type:** This is a highly aggressive lymphoma typically arising in the nasal cavity or other extranodal sites (skin, GI tract). It is strongly associated with EBV infection (nearly 100% of cases). Histologically, it features a diffuse infiltrate of atypical lymphoid cells, often with prominent necrosis and angiocentricity. Immunophenotypically, it expresses T-cell markers (CD3) and/or NK-cell markers (CD56), often with co-expression. The EBV association is mediated by viral proteins like LMP1, which promote cell proliferation and survival.
*   **Angioimmunoblastic T-cell lymphoma (AITL):** This is another aggressive T-cell lymphoma, often presenting with systemic symptoms, skin rash, lymphadenopathy, and hepatosplenomegaly. It is characterized by a polymorphous infiltrate with numerous vascular proliferations (angiocentricity). AITL can arise de novo or transform from other T-cell lymphomas, including NK/T-cell lymphomas. Chronic immunosuppression (e.g., post-transplant, autoimmune disease treatment) is a known risk factor for AITL. The transformation from NK/T-cell lymphoma to AITL is a recognized phenomenon, particularly in patients with underlying immunosuppression or autoimmune conditions like SLE.
*   **SLE and Azathioprine:** SLE is an autoimmune disease, and azathioprine is an immunosuppressant used to treat it. Both conditions increase the risk of developing certain lymphomas, particularly EBV-associated ones like NK/T-cell lymphoma and post-transplant lymphoproliferative disorders (PTLDs), which share similarities with AITL. The chronic immunosuppression can lead to EBV reactivation and potentially drive the development or transformation of lymphoma.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Diffuse large B-cell lymphoma (DLBCL), transformed from a pre-existing follicular lymphoma, complicated by EBV reactivation due to immunosuppression.** Incorrect. The immunophenotype (CD3+, CD56+, CD19-, PAX5-) is inconsistent with a B-cell lymphoma (DLBCL or follicular lymphoma). DLBCL typically expresses B-cell markers (CD19, CD20, PAX5) and lacks CD56. While EBV can be associated with DLBCL in immunosuppressed patients, the specific immunophenotype rules this out.
*   **(B) Burkitt lymphoma, arising de novo in the nasal cavity, associated with c-MYC translocation and EBV infection.** Incorrect. Burkitt lymphoma is a high-grade B-cell lymphoma. The immunophenotype (CD3+, CD56+, CD19-, PAX5-) is inconsistent with Burkitt lymphoma. While Burkitt lymphoma is associated with EBV and c-MYC translocation, it is a B-cell malignancy.
*   **(D) Peripheral T-cell lymphoma, not otherwise specified (PTCL-NOS), arising secondary to chronic inflammation from SLE, characterized by CD3 positivity and lack of specific markers.** Incorrect. While PTCL-NOS is a possibility for a CD3+ T-cell lymphoma without specific features, the combination of nasal location, extensive necrosis, angiocentricity, CD56 positivity, and strong EBV association strongly points towards NK/T-cell lymphoma, nasal type. PTCL-NOS is typically a diagnosis of exclusion and less likely given the specific features presented. Furthermore, this option doesn't address the potential transformation into AITL, which is a key consideration given the patient's history.
*   **(E) Anaplastic large cell lymphoma (ALCL), positive for CD30 and ALK, presenting with nasal mass and systemic symptoms.** Incorrect. ALCL is typically positive for CD30 but usually also expresses T-cell markers (CD3) or is ALK-positive (in the ALK+ subtype) or CD138+ (in the ALK- subtype). While CD30 is positive in this case, the immunophenotype (CD3+, CD56+, CD19-, PAX5-) and the strong EBV association are more characteristic of NK/T-cell lymphoma. ALK positivity is not mentioned, and ALCL is less commonly associated with EBV and the nasal cavity compared to NK/T-cell lymphoma.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | Extranodal NK/T-cell Lymphoma, Nasal Type | AITL                                     | DLBCL                                  | Burkitt Lymphoma                       | PTCL-NOS                             | ALCL                                     |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------ | :--------------------------------------- |
| **Origin**              | Nasal cavity/Extranodal                   | Lymph nodes/Extranodal                   | Lymph nodes/Extranodal                 | Jaw, abdomen, lymph nodes              | Lymph nodes/Extranodal                 | Lymph nodes/Extranodal                 |
| **Cell Lineage**        | NK/T-cell                                 | T-cell                                   | B-cell                                 | B-cell                                 | T-cell                                | T-cell/ALK+ or CD138+                   |
| **Key Markers**         | CD3+, CD56+, CD30+, EBV+                   | CD3+, CD4-, CD8-, CD30+, LMP1+, EBV+     | CD19+, CD20+, CD79a+, PAX5+             | CD19+, CD20+, CD10+, c-MYC+             | CD3+ (variable other markers)          | CD30+, ALK+ or CD138+                   |
| **Histology**           | Diffuse infiltrate, necrosis, angiocentricity | Polymorphous, vascular proliferation     | Diffuse sheets, variable architecture | Starry sky, diffuse sheets             | Variable, often diffuse               | Sheets of large cells, syncytial growth |
| **EBV Association**     | Very Strong (90-100%)                     | Strong (30-50%)                          | Variable (esp. immunosuppressed)       | Variable (esp. endemic form)           | Variable                               | Rare                                     |
| **Nasal Involvement**   | Common (Primary site)                     | Rare                                      | Uncommon                               | Rare                                     | Uncommon                               | Rare                                     |
| **Association w/ SLE/Immuno** | Yes                                      | Yes (Risk factor for de novo/transformation) | Yes (Risk factor)                     | No specific association               | Yes (Risk factor)                     | No specific association               |
| **Transformation Risk** | Yes (e.g., to AITL)                       | N/A                                      | Yes (e.g., from FL)                    | Rare                                     | Yes                                   | Rare                                     |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Extranodal NK/T-cell lymphoma, nasal type, is strongly associated with EBV and presents with destructive nasal lesions. Its immunophenotype (CD3+, CD56+) is key. Chronic immunosuppression (like azathioprine for SLE) increases the risk of EBV-associated lymphomas and potential transformation to other aggressive types like AITL. Differentiating it from other lymphomas requires careful attention to morphology, immunophenotype, and clinical context. Key differentials include other T-cell lymphomas (PTCL-NOS, AITL), B-cell lymphomas (DLBCL, Burkitt), and ALCL.

<a id='question-140'></a>

### Question 140: Hematopoietic Pathology: Leukemias and Lymphomas: Focus 03-40 (disease (e.g., Sjögren disease, Hashimoto thyroidi)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the understanding of the characteristic cytogenetic abnormality (t(14;18) translocation) and its molecular consequence (IGH-BCL2 fusion protein) in Follicular Lymphoma, and its distinction from other B-cell lymphomas with similar clinical presentations or cytogenetic features.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old woman presents to her primary care physician with painless swelling of lymph nodes in her neck and axillae, which she noticed several months ago. She denies fever, night sweats, or weight loss (B symptoms). Physical examination reveals multiple, firm, non-tender, rubbery lymph nodes ranging from 1 to 3 cm in diameter. Her spleen is palpable 2 cm below the left costal margin. Complete blood count (CBC) shows mild normocytic anemia (Hgb 11.5 g/dL, normal range 12-16 g/dL) and normal white blood cell count (WBC 6,500/µL, normal range 4,000-11,000/µL) with a normal differential. A lymph node biopsy is performed. Histopathology shows effacement of the normal lymph node architecture by a proliferation of small to medium-sized lymphoid cells with round nuclei, vesicular chromatin, inconspicuous nucleoli, and scant cytoplasm. The cells are arranged in a follicular growth pattern, forming nodules separated by thin septa containing a network of capillaries (creating a "fishnet" appearance). Immunohistochemistry reveals the neoplastic cells are positive for CD10, CD19, CD20, and BCL2, and negative for CD5 and BCL6. Cytogenetic analysis of the tumor cells reveals a translocation between chromosomes 14 and 18, t(14;18)(q32;q21).

Which of the following molecular events is the most likely direct consequence of the cytogenetic abnormality found in this patient's lymph node biopsy?

(A) Formation of a fusion protein leading to constitutive activation of the MYC oncogene.
(B) Formation of a fusion protein leading to constitutive activation of the BCL2 anti-apoptotic protein.
(C) Formation of a fusion protein leading to constitutive activation of the BCL6 transcriptional repressor protein.
(D) Formation of a fusion protein leading to constitutive activation of the CD5 surface marker protein.
(E) Formation of a fusion protein leading to constitutive activation of the cyclin D1 protein.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old woman with painless, generalized lymphadenopathy and mild splenomegaly, without B symptoms. This presentation is typical for indolent (low-grade) B-cell lymphomas.
*   **Histopathology:** Effacement of lymph node architecture by small to medium-sized lymphoid cells in a follicular pattern ("fishnet" appearance). This morphology is highly characteristic of Follicular Lymphoma (FL).
*   **Immunohistochemistry:** Positive for CD10, CD19, CD20 (B-cell markers), and crucially, BCL2. Negative for CD5 (ruling out Mantle Cell Lymphoma) and BCL6 (ruling out Diffuse Large B-cell Lymphoma, which often arises from FL transformation). The BCL2 positivity is a key feature of FL.
*   **Cytogenetics:** t(14;18)(q32;q21). This specific translocation is the hallmark cytogenetic abnormality found in the vast majority (>85%) of Follicular Lymphomas.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the direct molecular consequence of the t(14;18) translocation.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   The t(14;18)(q32;q21) translocation juxtaposes the *BCL2* gene (located on chromosome 18) with the immunoglobulin heavy chain (IGH) locus (located on chromosome 14).
*   This juxtaposition leads to the overexpression of the *BCL2* gene.
*   The BCL2 protein is an anti-apoptotic protein. It inhibits programmed cell death (apoptosis) by preventing the release of cytochrome c from mitochondria and blocking the activation of caspases.
*   Overexpression of BCL2, driven by the t(14;18) translocation, leads to the survival of B lymphocytes that would normally undergo apoptosis. This results in the accumulation of these cells and the development of Follicular Lymphoma.
*   The resulting protein is a fusion protein where the BCL2 protein is constitutively active, preventing apoptosis and promoting cell survival.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Formation of a fusion protein leading to constitutive activation of the MYC oncogene.** This describes the t(8;14) translocation, characteristic of Burkitt Lymphoma. The *MYC* gene (chromosome 8) is juxtaposed with the IGH locus, leading to *MYC* overexpression and uncontrolled cell proliferation. This is incorrect for Follicular Lymphoma.
*   **(C) Formation of a fusion protein leading to constitutive activation of the BCL6 transcriptional repressor protein.** BCL6 is a transcriptional repressor normally expressed in germinal center B cells. While BCL6 is often positive in Diffuse Large B-cell Lymphoma (DLBCL), its overexpression is not typically caused by a specific translocation in FL. The t(14;18) involves BCL2, not BCL6. This is incorrect.
*   **(D) Formation of a fusion protein leading to constitutive activation of the CD5 surface marker protein.** CD5 is a surface marker typically found on T cells and a subset of B cells (e.g., Mantle Cell Lymphoma, Chronic Lymphocytic Leukemia). The t(14;18) does not involve the CD5 gene. Mantle Cell Lymphoma is characterized by the t(11;14) translocation involving the *CCND1* gene (cyclin D1) and the IGH locus. This is incorrect.
*   **(E) Formation of a fusion protein leading to constitutive activation of the cyclin D1 protein.** This describes the t(11;14) translocation, characteristic of Mantle Cell Lymphoma. The *CCND1* gene (encoding cyclin D1) on chromosome 11 is juxtaposed with the IGH locus, leading to cyclin D1 overexpression, which drives cell cycle progression. This is incorrect for Follicular Lymphoma.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Follicular Lymphoma (FL) | Burkitt Lymphoma (BL) | Mantle Cell Lymphoma (MCL) | Diffuse Large B-cell Lymphoma (DLBCL) |
| :------------------ | :----------------------- | :-------------------- | :------------------------- | :------------------------------------ |
| **Typical Age**     | Middle-aged/Elderly      | Children/Young Adults | Middle-aged/Elderly        | Variable                              |
| **Clinical**        | Indolent, painless LAD   | Aggressive, rapid growth | Indolent/Aggressive        | Aggressive                            |
| **Histology**       | Follicular pattern       | Diffuse, starry sky   | Mantle zone effacement     | Diffuse sheets                        |
| **Immunophenotype** | CD10+, BCL2+, BCL6-      | CD10+, BCL2-, BCL6+    | CD5+, Cyclin D1+           | CD10+, BCL2+, BCL6+ (variable)        |
| **Cytogenetics**    | t(14;18)                 | t(8;14)               | t(11;14)                   | Variable, often t(14;18) or +3        |
| **Molecular**       | IGH-BCL2 fusion          | IGH-MYC fusion        | IGH-CCND1 fusion           | Variable                              |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The t(14;18) translocation is the defining genetic abnormality in Follicular Lymphoma, leading to overexpression of the anti-apoptotic BCL2 protein via the IGH-BCL2 fusion. This promotes the survival of neoplastic B cells. Remember to differentiate this translocation from t(8;14) in Burkitt Lymphoma (MYC) and t(11;14) in Mantle Cell Lymphoma (Cyclin D1).

<a id='question-141'></a>

### Question 141: Hematopoietic Pathology: Leukemias and Lymphomas: Focus 03-41 (lymphoma remains localized for long periods of time)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the clinical presentation, diagnostic features, and characteristic progression patterns of different types of lymphoma, specifically distinguishing between indolent (slow-growing) and aggressive (fast-growing) lymphomas, and recognizing the significance of specific cytogenetic abnormalities in lymphoma prognosis. (理解不同类型淋巴瘤的临床表现、诊断特征和特征性进展模式，特别是区分惰性（缓慢生长）和侵袭性（快速生长）淋巴瘤，并认识到细胞遗传学异常在淋巴瘤预后中的意义。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male presents to his primary care physician for a routine check-up. He reports feeling generally well but mentions a painless, slowly enlarging mass in his left neck that he first noticed about 6 months ago. He denies fever, night sweats, unintentional weight loss (B symptoms), or recent infections. His past medical history is significant for hypertension and hyperlipidemia, both well-controlled with medication. Physical examination reveals a firm, non-tender, mobile lymph node measuring approximately 4 cm in diameter in the left supraclavicular region. No other lymphadenopathy is noted. Complete blood count (CBC) shows a normal white blood cell count (7.5 x 10^9/L) with a normal differential, hemoglobin (14.0 g/dL), and platelet count (250 x 10^9/L). A CT scan of the neck, chest, abdomen, and pelvis reveals localized lymphadenopathy confined to the left supraclavicular region, with no evidence of splenomegaly or hepatomegaly. An excisional biopsy of the affected lymph node is performed. Histopathology shows effacement of the normal lymph node architecture by a monotonous proliferation of small, mature-appearing lymphocytes with round nuclei, clumped chromatin, and scant cytoplasm. The cells are positive for CD5 and CD19, and negative for CD10 and CD20. Cytogenetic analysis reveals a translocation t(11;14)(q13;q32).

Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis?

(A) Diffuse Large B-cell Lymphoma (DLBCL)
(B) Follicular Lymphoma
(C) Mantle Cell Lymphoma
(D) Burkitt Lymphoma
(E) Classical Hodgkin Lymphoma

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male. Age is relevant as certain lymphomas are more common in specific age groups.
*   **Presentation:** Painless, slowly enlarging neck mass (lymphadenopathy) for 6 months. This suggests a chronic, indolent process rather than an acute, aggressive one. The absence of B symptoms (fever, night sweats, weight loss) further supports an indolent course.
*   **Physical Exam:** Firm, non-tender, mobile lymph node (4 cm) in the left supraclavicular region. Localized lymphadenopathy without systemic involvement.
*   **Labs:** Normal CBC. This is common in indolent lymphomas, where the bone marrow is often spared initially.
*   **Imaging:** CT scan shows localized lymphadenopathy, no splenomegaly or hepatomegaly. This confirms the localized nature of the disease.
*   **Histopathology:** Effacement of lymph node architecture by monotonous proliferation of small, mature-appearing lymphocytes. This morphology points towards a low-grade lymphoma.
*   **Immunophenotype:** CD5+, CD19+, CD10-, CD20-. CD5 positivity in B-cells is characteristic of certain B-cell lymphomas, including CLL/SLL and Mantle Cell Lymphoma. CD10 negativity helps exclude Follicular Lymphoma. CD20 negativity (or dim positivity) can be seen in Mantle Cell Lymphoma.
*   **Cytogenetics:** t(11;14)(q13;q32). This translocation involves the *CCND1* gene (cyclin D1) on chromosome 11 and the immunoglobulin heavy chain locus on chromosome 14. This is the hallmark genetic abnormality of Mantle Cell Lymphoma.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Mantle Cell Lymphoma (MCL):** This is a B-cell non-Hodgkin lymphoma characterized by the t(11;14) translocation, leading to overexpression of cyclin D1. Cyclin D1 promotes cell cycle progression by inactivating the retinoblastoma protein (pRb), leading to uncontrolled proliferation of mantle zone B-cells.
*   **Clinical Course:** MCL typically presents in older adults (median age ~60s) with generalized lymphadenopathy, splenomegaly, and often bone marrow involvement at diagnosis. However, it can present as localized disease, especially in the early stages, as seen in this case. While often considered aggressive, it can have an indolent phase, especially the leukemic non-nodal variant. The classic presentation involves lymphadenopathy, splenomegaly, and bone marrow involvement.
*   **Histology:** Characterized by small to medium-sized lymphocytes with irregular nuclear contours and clumped chromatin.
*   **Immunophenotype:** Typically CD5+, CD19+, CD20+ (often dim), CD23-, CD10-. The CD5+ phenotype is shared with CLL/SLL but distinguishes it from Follicular Lymphoma.
*   **Cytogenetics:** The t(11;14)(q13;q32) translocation is present in >95% of cases, leading to *CCND1* overexpression.
*   **Progression:** MCL often progresses from an indolent phase to a more aggressive blastoid phase.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Diffuse Large B-cell Lymphoma (DLBCL):** This is the most common type of non-Hodgkin lymphoma. It is typically aggressive, presenting with rapidly enlarging lymph nodes, B symptoms, and often extranodal involvement. Histology shows large B-cells with prominent nucleoli. Immunophenotype is typically CD19+, CD20+, CD10 variable, CD5-. Cytogenetics are variable, but t(11;14) is not characteristic. The slow progression and small cell morphology in the vignette are inconsistent with DLBCL.
*   **(B) Follicular Lymphoma:** This is the second most common type of non-Hodgkin lymphoma. It is typically indolent, often presenting with widespread, painless lymphadenopathy. Histology shows a follicular growth pattern with centrocytes and centroblasts. Immunophenotype is typically CD19+, CD20+, CD10+, CD5-, BCL2+. The t(14;18) translocation involving *BCL2* is characteristic. The small cell morphology, CD5+ phenotype, and t(11;14) translocation are inconsistent with Follicular Lymphoma.
*   **(D) Burkitt Lymphoma:** This is a highly aggressive B-cell lymphoma. It typically presents with rapidly growing masses, often extranodal (e.g., jaw, abdomen). Histology shows monotonous medium-sized cells with round nuclei, multiple nucleoli, and a "starry sky" pattern. Immunophenotype is typically CD19+, CD20+, CD10+, BCL6+, BCL2-. Cytogenetics involve translocations involving the *MYC* gene on chromosome 8 (e.g., t(8;14)). The slow progression, small cell morphology, and t(11;14) translocation are inconsistent with Burkitt Lymphoma.
*   **(E) Classical Hodgkin Lymphoma:** This lymphoma is characterized by the presence of Reed-Sternberg cells (large, multinucleated cells) in a background of reactive inflammatory cells. It often presents with localized lymphadenopathy, frequently in the cervical region, and may be associated with B symptoms. Immunophenotype of Reed-Sternberg cells is typically CD30+, CD15+, CD20-. The monotonous proliferation of small lymphocytes without Reed-Sternberg cells and the specific immunophenotype (CD5+, CD19+) and cytogenetics (t(11;14)) are inconsistent with Hodgkin Lymphoma.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature             | Mantle Cell Lymphoma (MCL) | DLBCL                     | Follicular Lymphoma       | Burkitt Lymphoma         | Hodgkin Lymphoma         |
| :------------------ | :------------------------- | :------------------------ | :----------------------- | :----------------------- | :----------------------- |
| **Clinical Course** | Indolent/Aggressive        | Aggressive                | Indolent                 | Aggressive               | Variable (often indolent) |
| **Cell Morphology** | Small-medium, irregular nuclei | Large, prominent nucleoli | Follicular pattern, centroblasts/centrocytes | Medium, starry sky       | Reed-Sternberg cells     |
| **Immunophenotype** | CD5+, CD19+, CD20+ (dim), CD10- | CD19+, CD20+, CD10 var, CD5- | CD19+, CD20+, CD10+, CD5-, BCL2+ | CD19+, CD20+, CD10+, BCL6+ | RS cells: CD30+, CD15+, CD20- |
| **Cytogenetics**    | t(11;14) (*CCND1*)         | Variable                  | t(14;18) (*BCL2*)         | t(8;14) (*MYC*)           | -                       |
| **Age**             | Older adults               | Variable                  | Older adults              | Children/Young Adults    | Bimodal (young, old)     |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Mantle Cell Lymphoma is characterized by the t(11;14) translocation leading to cyclin D1 overexpression, typically presenting in older adults with lymphadenopathy. Key distinguishing features include a CD5+ immunophenotype in B-cells and the specific cytogenetic abnormality. Differentiating it from other lymphomas like DLBCL (large cells, aggressive), Follicular Lymphoma (CD10+, t(14;18)), and Burkitt Lymphoma (starry sky, t(8;14)) relies on morphology, immunophenotype, and cytogenetics.

<a id='question-142'></a>

### Question 142: Hematopoietic Pathology: Leukemias and Lymphomas: Focus 03-42 (Albumin)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the pathophysiology, clinical presentation, and diagnostic features of multiple myeloma, specifically focusing on the role of monoclonal light chains (Bence Jones proteins) in causing renal damage (cast nephropathy) and the characteristic laboratory findings. (考察多发性骨髓瘤的病理生理学、临床表现和诊断特征，特别是单克隆轻链（Bence Jones蛋白）导致肾脏损伤（铸型肾病）的作用和实验室发现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male presents to the emergency department with complaints of increasing fatigue, bone pain (especially in his lower back), and recent onset of lower extremity edema over the past month. His past medical history is significant for hypertension and type 2 diabetes mellitus. On physical examination, he appears pale and cachectic. Vital signs show a blood pressure of 145/90 mmHg, heart rate of 88 bpm, respiratory rate of 18/min, and temperature of 37.0°C. Laboratory investigations reveal:
- Hemoglobin: 9.5 g/dL (Reference range: 13.5-17.5 g/dL)
- Serum creatinine: 2.8 mg/dL (Reference range: 0.7-1.3 mg/dL)
- Serum calcium: 13.2 mg/dL (Reference range: 8.5-10.5 mg/dL)
- Serum albumin: 2.5 g/dL (Reference range: 3.5-5.0 g/dL)
- Urinalysis: 3+ protein, numerous amorphous eosinophilic casts.
- Serum protein electrophoresis (SPEP): Monoclonal spike in the gamma region.
- Immunofixation electrophoresis (IFE): Confirms monoclonal IgG kappa light chain.
- Urine protein electrophoresis (UPEP): Monoclonal kappa light chains detected (Bence Jones protein).
- Bone marrow biopsy: Shows 40% plasma cells, many atypical, with nuclear inclusions.
Based on these findings, what is the most likely mechanism underlying the patient's renal dysfunction and proteinuria?

(A) Amyloid deposition of light chains in the glomeruli, leading to nephrotic syndrome.
(B) Immune complex deposition involving monoclonal immunoglobulins, causing membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis.
(C) Precipitation of monoclonal light chains within the renal tubules, forming casts that obstruct tubular flow and cause tubular damage.
(D) Direct toxic effect of monoclonal immunoglobulins on podocytes, leading to focal segmental glomerulosclerosis (FSGS).
(E) Hypercalcemia-induced vasoconstriction and glomerular damage, resulting in acute kidney injury.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male with fatigue, bone pain, edema. Suggests a systemic illness, potentially hematologic or oncologic.
- **Symptoms:** Fatigue (anemia), bone pain (lytic lesions), edema (renal dysfunction, hypoalbuminemia).
- **Labs:**
    - Anemia (Hgb 9.5 g/dL): Common in multiple myeloma due to bone marrow infiltration and suppression of normal hematopoiesis.
    - Elevated Creatinine (2.8 mg/dL): Indicates renal dysfunction.
    - Hypercalcemia (13.2 mg/dL): Common in multiple myeloma due to osteoclast activation by tumor cells.
    - Hypoalbuminemia (2.5 g/dL): Can be due to chronic illness, renal loss, or decreased synthesis.
    - Proteinuria (3+): Suggests glomerular or tubular damage.
    - Amorphous eosinophilic casts: Highly suggestive of myeloma cast nephropathy.
- **Electrophoresis:** SPEP shows monoclonal spike (M-protein), IFE confirms IgG kappa, UPEP detects Bence Jones protein (kappa light chains). Confirms monoclonal gammopathy.
- **Bone Marrow Biopsy:** 40% plasma cells confirms plasma cell neoplasm.
- **Diagnosis:** The constellation of findings (anemia, bone pain, hypercalcemia, renal failure, monoclonal protein, plasma cell infiltration) is classic for multiple myeloma.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism of renal dysfunction and proteinuria in this context.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Multiple Myeloma Pathophysiology:** A malignancy of plasma cells characterized by the proliferation of a single clone of plasma cells in the bone marrow, leading to the production of a monoclonal immunoglobulin (M-protein) or light chains.
- **Renal Involvement in Multiple Myeloma:** Common (myeloma kidney). Several mechanisms exist:
    - **Cast Nephropathy (Myeloma Kidney):** This is the most common cause. Excess monoclonal free light chains (Bence Jones proteins), particularly kappa chains, are filtered by the glomerulus. In the acidic environment of the distal tubules and collecting ducts, these light chains precipitate with Tamm-Horsfall protein, forming large, eosinophilic casts. These casts obstruct the tubules, leading to tubular damage, interstitial inflammation, and ultimately renal failure. The proteinuria is primarily due to the presence of these light chains and albumin lost due to tubular damage. The amorphous eosinophilic casts seen on urinalysis are characteristic.
    - **Amyloidosis (AL Amyloidosis):** Monoclonal light chains can misfold and deposit as amyloid fibrils in various organs, including the kidneys. This typically causes nephrotic syndrome (heavy proteinuria, hypoalbuminemia, edema, hyperlipidemia) due to glomerular deposition. While possible, the presence of *amorphous eosinophilic casts* points more strongly towards cast nephropathy.
    - **Light Chain Deposition Disease (LCDD):** Non-amyloid deposition of monoclonal light chains in the glomeruli and tubules, leading to proteinuria and renal dysfunction.
    - **Immune Complex Glomerulonephritis:** Deposition of immune complexes containing monoclonal immunoglobulins, leading to various glomerulonephritis patterns (e.g., membranoproliferative).
    - **Hypercalcemia:** Can cause renal vasoconstriction, polyuria, and nephrocalcinosis, contributing to renal dysfunction.
    - **Direct Tubular Toxicity:** Light chains can be directly toxic to tubular cells.
- **Linking Vignette to Mechanism:** The patient has multiple myeloma (confirmed by SPEP, IFE, bone marrow biopsy). He has renal failure (elevated creatinine) and proteinuria (3+ protein, Bence Jones protein in urine). The urinalysis finding of *amorphous eosinophilic casts* is the key clue pointing specifically to myeloma cast nephropathy. This occurs when excess monoclonal light chains precipitate in the renal tubules, forming casts that cause obstruction and damage.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Amyloid deposition (AL amyloidosis) is a possible complication of multiple myeloma, but it typically presents with *nephrotic-range proteinuria* (often >3.5 g/day) and characteristic Congo red staining with apple-green birefringence under polarized light. The urinalysis finding of *amorphous eosinophilic casts* is much more characteristic of cast nephropathy, not amyloidosis. Amyloidosis usually causes a more "classic" nephrotic picture.
- (B): Immune complex deposition can occur but is less common than cast nephropathy. It typically presents with features of glomerulonephritis (hematuria, hypertension, varying degrees of proteinuria), often with specific histological patterns like membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis. The presence of amorphous eosinophilic casts strongly favors cast nephropathy over immune complex disease.
- (D): Direct toxic effects of monoclonal immunoglobulins on podocytes can occur, potentially leading to FSGS, but this is not the primary mechanism for the renal failure and proteinuria seen in most myeloma patients, especially when amorphous eosinophilic casts are present. Cast nephropathy is far more common.
- (E): Hypercalcemia is common in myeloma and can contribute to renal dysfunction (nephrocalcinosis, vasoconstriction), but it does not directly explain the significant proteinuria and the presence of amorphous eosinophilic casts. The casts are formed by the precipitation of light chains, not calcium. While hypercalcemia worsens renal function, it's not the primary cause of the proteinuria and cast formation described.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | Cast Nephropathy (Myeloma Kidney) | AL Amyloidosis                  | Immune Complex GN             | FSGS (due to M-protein) | Hypercalcemia Effect on Kidney |
|-------------------------|-----------------------------------|---------------------------------|---------------------------------|--------------------------|---------------------------------|
| **Primary Mechanism**   | Light chain precipitation in tubules | Amyloid fibril deposition       | Immune complex deposition      | Podocyte injury          | Vasoconstriction, nephrocalcinosis |
| **Proteinuria Type**    | Tubular (Bence Jones), Albuminuria | Nephrotic range (>3.5 g/day)    | Variable (Glomerular)          | Variable (Glomerular)    | Usually mild                    |
| **Urinalysis**          | Amorphous eosinophilic casts      | -                              | Hematuria, RBC casts (possible) | -                        | -                               |
| **Histology**           | Tubular obstruction, interstitial inflammation | Amyloid deposits (Congo red+) | Glomerular changes (e.g., MPGN) | Podocyte effacement     | Vasoconstriction, calcification |
| **Common in Myeloma?**  | Yes (Most common)                 | Yes                             | Less common                    | Less common              | Yes                             |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Multiple myeloma commonly causes renal failure, most often due to myeloma cast nephropathy resulting from the precipitation of monoclonal light chains (Bence Jones proteins) in the renal tubules, forming characteristic amorphous eosinophilic casts. Differentiate this from AL amyloidosis (nephrotic syndrome, Congo red positive), immune complex GN (glomerulonephritis features), and hypercalcemia-induced renal injury. The presence of amorphous eosinophilic casts is a key diagnostic clue for cast nephropathy.

<a id='question-143'></a>

### Question 143: Hematopoietic Pathology: Leukemias and Lymphomas: Focus 03-43 (Multiple myeloma is a malignant neoplasm of plasma cells)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Multiple myeloma is characterized by the clonal proliferation of malignant plasma cells in the bone marrow, leading to bone destruction, hypercalcemia, renal failure, and anemia. Diagnosis often involves serum/urine protein electrophoresis, immunofixation, and bone marrow biopsy showing >10% clonal plasma cells. (多发性骨髓瘤是骨髓中恶性浆细胞的克隆增殖，导致骨骼破坏、高钙血症、肾功能衰竭和贫血。诊断通常涉及血清/尿液蛋白电泳、免疫固定和骨髓活检，显示>10%的克隆浆细胞。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male presents to the emergency department complaining of severe lower back pain for the past 3 months, which has worsened significantly over the last week. He also reports increasing fatigue, constipation, and nocturia. His past medical history is significant for hypertension and osteoarthritis. Physical examination reveals tenderness over the lumbar spine and mild pallor. Vital signs show a blood pressure of 145/85 mmHg, heart rate of 88 bpm, respiratory rate of 16 breaths/min, and temperature of 37.0°C. Laboratory results show: Hemoglobin 9.5 g/dL (reference range: 13.5-17.5 g/dL), Serum calcium 13.2 mg/dL (reference range: 8.5-10.5 mg/dL), Serum creatinine 2.5 mg/dL (reference range: 0.6-1.2 mg/dL), Serum total protein 9.0 g/dL (reference range: 6.0-8.3 g/dL), Serum albumin 3.5 g/dL (reference range: 3.5-5.0 g/dL), Serum globulin 5.5 g/dL (reference range: 2.0-3.5 g/dL), Urine dipstick shows 3+ protein. Serum protein electrophoresis (SPEP) reveals a monoclonal spike (M-spike) in the gamma region. Immunofixation electrophoresis (IFE) confirms the presence of monoclonal IgG kappa light chains. A skeletal survey shows multiple lytic lesions throughout the axial skeleton. Bone marrow biopsy shows 45% clonal plasma cells.

What is the most likely diagnosis?

(A) Waldenström macroglobulinemia
(B) Amyloidosis
(C) Monoclonal gammopathy of undetermined significance (MGUS)
(D) Multiple myeloma
(E) Primary (AL) amyloidosis

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male. Age is typical for multiple myeloma.
- **Presenting Symptoms:** Severe lower back pain (bone pain), fatigue (anemia), constipation, nocturia (hypercalcemia, renal impairment). These are classic symptoms of multiple myeloma (CRAB criteria: Calcium elevation, Renal insufficiency, Anemia, Bone lesions).
- **Physical Exam:** Tenderness over lumbar spine (bone involvement), pallor (anemia).
- **Laboratory Findings:**
    - Anemia (Hgb 9.5 g/dL): Common in multiple myeloma due to bone marrow infiltration and renal impairment.
    - Hypercalcemia (Ca 13.2 mg/dL): Due to osteoclast activation by myeloma cells.
    - Renal insufficiency (Creatinine 2.5 mg/dL): Can be caused by hypercalcemia, light chain cast nephropathy, amyloidosis, or dehydration.
    - Elevated total protein (9.0 g/dL) with normal albumin (3.5 g/dL) and elevated globulin (5.5 g/dL): Suggests a monoclonal protein component.
    - Proteinuria (3+): Often due to Bence Jones proteins (light chains) in the urine.
    - SPEP shows M-spike: Indicates a monoclonal gammopathy.
    - IFE confirms monoclonal IgG kappa: Identifies the specific monoclonal immunoglobulin.
- **Imaging:** Skeletal survey shows multiple lytic lesions: Characteristic bone destruction in multiple myeloma.
- **Bone Marrow Biopsy:** 45% clonal plasma cells: Confirms significant plasma cell infiltration (>10% is diagnostic for multiple myeloma).
- **Synthesis:** The combination of CRAB criteria (hyperCalcemia, Renal failure, Anemia, Bone lesions), monoclonal protein (M-spike, IgG kappa), and significant bone marrow plasma cell infiltration (>10%) definitively points to multiple myeloma.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Pathophysiology:** Multiple myeloma is a malignancy of terminally differentiated B-lymphocytes (plasma cells). These malignant plasma cells proliferate uncontrollably in the bone marrow, producing large amounts of a monoclonal immunoglobulin (M-protein) or light chains.
- **Mechanism of CRAB Criteria:**
    - **C**alcium elevation: Myeloma cells secrete factors (e.g., RANKL, MIP-1α) that activate osteoclasts, leading to increased bone resorption and release of calcium.
    - **R**enal insufficiency: Can be caused by light chain cast nephropathy (Bence Jones proteins precipitate in renal tubules), hypercalcemia, amyloid deposition, or dehydration.
    - **A**nemia: Due to bone marrow infiltration by plasma cells, suppression of normal hematopoiesis, and effects of renal failure (decreased erythropoietin).
    - **B**one lesions: Osteolytic lesions result from the imbalance between bone resorption (stimulated by myeloma cells) and bone formation.
- **Molecular Aspects:** Genetic abnormalities are common, including translocations involving the immunoglobulin heavy chain locus (e.g., t(11;14), t(4;14), t(14;16)) and deletions (e.g., del(13q), del(17p)). These mutations often affect cell cycle regulation, apoptosis, and cytokine production.
- **Diagnosis:** Requires evidence of clonal plasma cell proliferation (e.g., bone marrow biopsy >10% plasma cells, or plasmacytoma) AND evidence of end-organ damage (CRAB criteria) OR specific myeloma-defining events (e.g., >60% bone marrow plasma cells, serum free light chain ratio ≥100, >1 focal lesion on MRI).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Waldenström macroglobulinemia: This is a lymphoplasmacytic lymphoma characterized by the production of monoclonal IgM. Patients typically present with hyperviscosity syndrome (due to high IgM levels), lymphadenopathy, splenomegaly, and anemia. Bone lesions and significant hypercalcemia are uncommon. The M-spike is IgM, not IgG kappa.
- (B) Amyloidosis: This involves the deposition of amyloid fibrils (often derived from monoclonal light chains in AL amyloidosis) in various organs. While it can be associated with multiple myeloma or MGUS, it is a distinct condition. The primary presentation here (bone pain, lytic lesions, hypercalcemia, renal failure, anemia, high plasma cells) is more characteristic of myeloma itself, although amyloidosis can be a complication. Amyloidosis alone wouldn't typically cause such prominent lytic bone lesions or >10% plasma cells unless it's AL amyloidosis secondary to myeloma.
- (C) Monoclonal gammopathy of undetermined significance (MGUS): This is a premalignant condition characterized by a monoclonal protein (M-spike) but without evidence of end-organ damage (CRAB criteria) or significant plasma cell infiltration (<10% in bone marrow). This patient clearly has end-organ damage (CRAB criteria) and significant bone marrow involvement (45% plasma cells), ruling out MGUS.
- (E) Primary (AL) amyloidosis: This is a systemic disease caused by the deposition of amyloid fibrils derived from immunoglobulin light chains produced by a clonal plasma cell population. While often associated with MGUS or smoldering myeloma, it can also occur with overt multiple myeloma. However, the defining features in this vignette are the CRAB criteria and the high percentage of plasma cells, making multiple myeloma the primary diagnosis. Amyloidosis would be a potential complication or associated finding, but not the overarching diagnosis explaining the entire clinical picture. The question asks for the *most likely diagnosis* explaining the constellation of findings.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | Multiple Myeloma (D)                                  | Waldenström Macroglobulinemia (A)                 | MGUS (C)                                        | AL Amyloidosis (E)                               |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------ | :---------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------ |
| **Cell Type**           | Malignant Plasma Cells                                | Lymphoplasmacytic Lymphocytes                      | Clonal Plasma Cells (<10%)                       | Clonal Plasma Cells (variable %)                  |
| **Monoclonal Protein**  | IgG, IgA, IgD, IgE (most commonly IgG or IgA), Light Chains | IgM                                               | IgG, IgA, IgE, Light Chains (usually low level) | Light Chains (most common)                       |
| **Bone Marrow Plasma Cells** | ≥10% (often >40%)                                    | Variable, usually <20%                             | <10%                                            | Variable, often <10% unless associated with MM    |
| **Bone Lesions**        | Common (lytic)                                        | Uncommon                                          | Uncommon                                        | Uncommon unless associated with MM                |
| **Hypercalcemia**       | Common                                                | Uncommon                                          | Uncommon                                        | Uncommon                                         |
| **Renal Failure**       | Common (light chain cast nephropathy)                  | Uncommon                                          | Uncommon                                        | Common (amyloid deposition)                      |
| **Anemia**              | Common                                                | Common                                            | Common                                          | Common                                           |
| **CRAB Criteria**       | Present (Calcium, Renal, Anemia, Bone)                 | Usually Absent                                    | Absent                                          | May be present (renal failure), but not typical MM |
| **M-spike**             | Present, often large                                   | Present, IgM                                       | Present, usually small                          | Often absent or small, light chain restriction   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Multiple myeloma is diagnosed based on the presence of clonal plasma cell proliferation (>10% in bone marrow or plasmacytoma) and evidence of end-organ damage (CRAB criteria) or myeloma-defining events. Differentiating it from MGUS requires evidence of end-organ damage or significant plasma cell burden. Waldenström macroglobulinemia involves IgM production and lymphoplasmacytic cells. AL amyloidosis involves light chain deposition and can be associated with myeloma or MGUS but is not the primary diagnosis when classic myeloma features are present.

<a id='question-144'></a>

### Question 144: Hematopoietic Pathology: Leukemias and Lymphomas
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Identify the most common primary malignant neoplasm of bone, its characteristic features, and associated clinical presentation. (鉴别骨骼最常见原发性恶性肿瘤的特征及临床表现)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 65-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department complaining of severe, localized bone pain in his right hip for the past 3 months, which has progressively worsened and is now constant, even at rest. He denies any history of trauma. He reports recent unintentional weight loss of approximately 10 pounds over the last 2 months and increasing fatigue. Physical examination reveals tenderness to palpation over the greater trochanter of the right hip. Neurological examination is unremarkable. Laboratory studies show elevated serum calcium (13.5 mg/dL, normal range 8.5-10.5 mg/dL), elevated alkaline phosphatase (350 U/L, normal range 30-120 U/L), and normal serum protein electrophoresis. A plain radiograph of the right hip shows a lytic lesion in the femoral neck with surrounding sclerosis and periosteal reaction ("onion skin" appearance). A bone biopsy is performed. Histopathological examination reveals sheets of atypical plasma cells with eccentric nuclei, prominent nucleoli, and abundant basophilic cytoplasm. Immunohistochemical staining shows positivity for CD138 and CD38, and negativity for CD45. Cytogenetic analysis reveals a t(11;14) translocation.

Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis?

(A) Ewing sarcoma
(B) Osteosarcoma
(C) Giant cell tumor of bone
(D) Multiple myeloma
(E) Metastatic prostate cancer

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Demographics & History:** 65-year-old male. Age is relevant as multiple myeloma is more common in older adults. History of hypertension and hyperlipidemia are common comorbidities but not directly diagnostic.
*   **Chief Complaint:** Severe, localized bone pain (right hip) worsening over 3 months, constant, even at rest. This is a classic symptom of bone destruction, often seen in primary or metastatic bone tumors, or conditions like multiple myeloma. Pain at rest suggests significant bone involvement.
*   **Associated Symptoms:** Unintentional weight loss and fatigue. These are constitutional symptoms often associated with malignancy.
*   **Physical Exam:** Tenderness over the greater trochanter. Localizes the pain source.
*   **Laboratory Findings:**
    *   Elevated serum calcium (hypercalcemia): Common complication of multiple myeloma due to osteoclast activation by tumor cells.
    *   Elevated alkaline phosphatase: Suggests increased bone turnover, seen in both bone formation and destruction.
    *   Normal serum protein electrophoresis: While monoclonal gammopathy is characteristic of multiple myeloma, serum protein electrophoresis can be normal in a subset of cases (especially light chain myeloma or oligoclonal myeloma). Further testing like serum free light chain assay or immunofixation would be needed. However, the other findings strongly point towards myeloma.
*   **Radiographic Findings:** Lytic lesion in the femoral neck with surrounding sclerosis and periosteal reaction ("onion skin" appearance). Lytic lesions are characteristic of multiple myeloma. While "onion skin" periosteal reaction is more typical of Ewing sarcoma, it can occasionally be seen in other bone lesions. The overall picture of a lytic lesion in an older adult with hypercalcemia is highly suggestive of myeloma.
*   **Bone Biopsy Findings:**
    *   Sheets of atypical plasma cells: This is the hallmark of plasma cell neoplasms.
    *   Eccentric nuclei, prominent nucleoli, abundant basophilic cytoplasm: Morphological features consistent with plasma cells.
    *   Immunohistochemistry: CD138 and CD38 positive, CD45 negative. CD138 and CD38 are plasma cell markers. CD45 (leukocyte common antigen) is typically negative in plasma cells, helping distinguish them from reactive lymphocytes or leukemic infiltrates.
    *   Cytogenetics: t(11;14) translocation. This translocation involves the *CCND1* gene (cyclin D1) on chromosome 11 and the immunoglobulin heavy chain locus (*IGH*) on chromosome 14. It is found in approximately 15-20% of multiple myeloma cases and is associated with a more aggressive clinical course.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Multiple Myeloma Pathophysiology:** Multiple myeloma is a malignant proliferation of plasma cells in the bone marrow. These neoplastic plasma cells produce monoclonal immunoglobulins (M-protein) or light chains, which can be detected in serum or urine. The tumor cells secrete factors (e.g., RANKL, MIP-1α) that activate osteoclasts, leading to bone resorption, lytic lesions, hypercalcemia, and bone pain. They also suppress osteoblast function, contributing to bone destruction. The accumulation of plasma cells in the bone marrow leads to bone marrow failure, causing anemia, thrombocytopenia, and leukopenia. Renal failure can occur due to light chain deposition (cast nephropathy) or hypercalcemia.
*   **Molecular Basis:** The t(11;14) translocation leads to overexpression of cyclin D1, promoting cell cycle progression and proliferation of the plasma cells. Other common translocations include t(4;14) involving *FGFR3* and *IGH*, and t(14;16) involving *MAF* and *IGH*.
*   **Clinical Manifestations:** The classic CRAB criteria (Hyper**C**alcemia, **R**enal insufficiency, **A**nemia, **B**one lesions) are key features. Bone pain is the most common presenting symptom.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Ewing sarcoma:** This is a malignant bone tumor primarily affecting children and young adults (peak incidence 10-20 years). It typically arises in the diaphysis of long bones or pelvis. Radiographically, it often presents with a permeative or "onion skin" periosteal reaction. Histologically, it consists of small, round, blue cells. The patient's age (65) and the histological description (plasma cells) make this diagnosis highly unlikely.
- (B) **Osteosarcoma:** This is the most common primary malignant bone tumor, typically occurring in adolescents and young adults (bimodal distribution with a smaller peak in older adults). It usually arises in the metaphysis of long bones (e.g., distal femur, proximal tibia). Radiographically, it often shows a destructive lesion with osteoid production (sclerosis). Histologically, it is characterized by malignant osteoblasts producing osteoid. The patient's age is less typical (though possible), and the histology (plasma cells) is completely inconsistent.
- (C) **Giant cell tumor of bone:** This is a locally aggressive but typically benign tumor, most common in young adults (20-40 years). It usually occurs in the epiphysis of long bones. Radiographically, it appears as an eccentric, lytic lesion extending to the subchondral bone. Histologically, it is characterized by mononuclear stromal cells and numerous multinucleated giant cells. The patient's age and the malignant nature suggested by the clinical picture (weight loss, hypercalcemia, atypical cells) rule this out.
- (E) **Metastatic prostate cancer:** Prostate cancer is a common malignancy in older men and frequently metastasizes to bone. Bone metastases are often osteoblastic (sclerotic) but can be lytic or mixed. While the patient's age and bone pain are consistent, the histology showing sheets of plasma cells, the specific immunohistochemical profile (CD138+, CD45-), and the t(11;14) translocation are diagnostic for multiple myeloma, not metastatic prostate cancer (which would show prostatic adenocarcinoma cells, PSA positive). Hypercalcemia can occur with bone metastases, but the overall picture strongly favors myeloma.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | Multiple Myeloma (Correct) | Ewing Sarcoma | Osteosarcoma | Giant Cell Tumor | Metastatic Prostate Cancer |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------- | :------------ | :----------- | :--------------- | :------------------------- |
| **Typical Age**         | >60 years                  | <20 years     | <20 years    | 20-40 years      | >50 years                  |
| **Primary Site**        | Bone Marrow                | Bone          | Bone         | Bone             | Prostate (Metastasis)      |
| **Radiology**           | Lytic lesions              | Lytic/Onion Skin | Lytic/Sclerotic | Lytic (Epiphyseal) | Osteoblastic/Lytic/Mixed   |
| **Histology**           | Plasma cells               | Small blue cells | Osteoid-producing cells | Giant cells + Stromal cells | Adenocarcinoma cells      |
| **Key Markers**         | CD138+, CD38+, CD45-       | CD99+         | N/A          | N/A              | PSA+                       |
| **Common Translocation** | t(11;14), t(4;14), t(14;16) | t(11;22)      | N/A          | N/A              | N/A                       |
| **Hypercalcemia**       | Common                     | Rare          | Rare         | Rare             | Common                     |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Multiple myeloma is the most common primary malignancy of bone in adults, characterized by plasma cell proliferation, lytic bone lesions, hypercalcemia, renal failure, and anemia. Distinguish it from other bone tumors like Ewing sarcoma (younger age, small blue cells, t(11;22)), osteosarcoma (younger age, osteoid production), giant cell tumor (benign, younger age, giant cells), and metastatic disease (histology based on primary site). Remember the CRAB criteria and the characteristic plasma cell morphology and immunophenotype.
